                                                  Doubly transitive equiangular tight frames that contain regular simplices

                                                                                              Matthew Fickus, Evan C. Lake
                                                            Department of Mathematics and Statistics, Air Force Institute of Technology, Wright–Patterson AFB, OH 45433




                                           Abstract
arXiv:2302.08879v2 [math.FA] 21 Jul 2025




                                           An equiangular tight frame (ETF) is a finite sequence of equal norm vectors in a Hilbert space that achieves equality
                                           in the Welch bound, and so has minimal coherence. The binder of an ETF is the set of all subsets of its indices whose
                                           corresponding vectors form a regular simplex. An ETF achieves equality in Donoho and Elad’s spark bound if and
                                           only if its binder is nonempty. When this occurs, its binder is the set of all linearly dependent subsets of it of minimal
                                           size. Moreover, if members of the binder form a balanced incomplete block design (BIBD) then its incidence matrix
                                           can be phased to produce a sparse representation of its dual (Naimark complement). A few infinite families of ETFs
                                           are known to have this remarkable property. In this paper, we relate this property to the recently introduced concept
                                           of a doubly transitive equiangular tight frame (DTETF), namely an ETF for which the natural action of its symmetry
                                           group is doubly transitive. In particular, we show that the binder of any DTETF is either empty or forms a BIBD, and
                                           moreover that when the latter occurs, any member of the binder of its dual is an oval of this BIBD. We then apply
                                           this general theory to certain known infinite families of DTETFs. Specifically, any symplectic form on a finite vector
                                           space yields a DTETF, and we compute the binder of it and its dual, showing that the former is empty except in a
                                           single notable case, and that the latter consists of affine Lagrangian subspaces. This unifies and generalizes several
                                           results from the existing literature. We then consider the binders of four infinite families of DTETFs that arise from
                                           quadratic forms over the field of two elements, showing that two of these are empty except in a finite number of cases,
                                           whereas the other two form BIBDs that relate to each other, and to Lagrangian subspaces, in nonobvious ways.
                                           Keywords: equiangular tight frame, regular simplex, doubly transitive
                                           2020 MSC: 42C15



                                           1. Introduction
                                               Let 𝜱 := (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N be a finite sequence of 𝑁 nonzero vectors in a Hilbert space E over F ∈ {R, C} of dimension
                                           𝐷 < 𝑁. Broadly speaking, the goal of compressed sensing is to uniquely recover, in a practical way, a vector
                                           𝒙 ∈ F N from 𝒚 = 𝜱𝒙 := 𝑛∈ N 𝒙(𝑛)𝝋 𝑛 subject to the assumption that 𝒙 is sufficiently sparse, namely that ∥𝒙∥ 0 :=
                                                                       Í
                                           #{𝑛 ∈ N : 𝒙(𝑛) ≠ 0} is sufficiently small. Since two sparse solutions of 𝜱𝒙 = 𝒚 differby a (possibly less) sparse
                                           member of ker(𝜱) := {𝒙 ∈ F N : 𝜱𝒙 = 0}, this can only happen if spark(𝜱) := min ∥𝒙∥ 0 : 0 ≠ 𝒙 ∈ ker(𝜱) is
                                           sufficiently large, that is, if every 𝐾-vector subsequence 𝜱 K := (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ K of 𝜱 is linearly independent for some
                                           sufficiently large 𝐾. Remarkably, a sufficiently sparse 𝒙 can be recovered from 𝒚 = 𝜱𝒙 using convex optimization
                                           when 𝜱 has the restricted isometry property (RIP), namely when every such subsequence 𝜱 K is approximately
                                           orthogonal in a particular way [10]. Though randomly chosen 𝜱 are likely to have the RIP to a remarkable degree, it
                                           is computationally hard to certify this fact for a general 𝜱 [3]. Computing the spark of a general 𝜱 is also hard [1].
                                           The problem of designing a deterministic 𝜱 that is guaranteed to have the RIP to a degree similar to that of its random
                                           cousins remains open [6, 4]. Much of the recent work on this problem is motivated by two bounds involving the
                                           coherence of 𝜱, the Welch bound [37] and Donoho and Elad’s spark bound [14], namely
                                                                                                                  1

if 𝐾 < 2 [coh(𝜱)] + 1 . Moreover, assuming without loss of generality that 𝜱 is equal norm, it is well known [34]
that it achieves equality in the Welch bound—and thus has minimal coherence—if and only if it is an equiangular
                                                                                     Í
tight frame (ETF) for E, namely if and only if there exists both 𝐴 > 0 such that 𝑛∈ N ⟨𝝋 𝑛 , 𝒚⟩𝝋 𝑛 = 𝐴𝒚 for all 𝒚 ∈ E
and 𝐶 > 0 such that |⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩| = 𝐶 when 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 . Any such 𝜱 has a dual (Naimark complement) 𝜳 = (𝝍 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N
that is an ETF for a space of dimension 𝑁 − 𝐷 with ⟨𝝍 𝑛1 , 𝝍 𝑛2 ⟩ = −⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩ whenever 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 , and that is unique
up to unitary transformations. Infinite families of ETFs are known, arising for example from algebro-combinatorial
designs. See [23] for a survey of ETFs.
     The binder of an ETF 𝜱 is the set bin(𝜱) of all subsets K of N for which 𝜱 K is a regular simplex, that is, a
𝐾-vector ETF for some (𝐾 − 1)-dimensional subspace of E. This set bin(𝜱) gives insight into the efficacy of 𝜱 for
compressed sensing: bin(𝜱) is nonempty if and only if 𝜱 achieves equality in the spark bound, and when this occurs,
bin(𝜱) consists of the smallest subsets K of N for which 𝜱 K is linearly dependent [16]. Moreover, if members of
bin(𝜱) form a balanced incomplete block design (BIBD), the incidence matrix of this BIBD can be phased to produce
a sparse representation of its dual 𝜳 [20, 16]. A few infinite families of ETFs are known to have this remarkable
property [20, 16, 5], and numerical experimentation gives various other small examples that do so as well [16].
     In this paper, we better explain why so many ETFs have binders that form BIBDs. The key idea is the recent
realization [27, 28, 12] that many ETFs are doubly transitive, that is, have the property that any distinct pair of its
indices can be mapped to any other such pair via the natural action of its symmetry group. In the next section, we
establish notation and review known facts that we will need later on. In Section 3, we show that the binder of any
doubly transitive equiangular tight frame (DTETF) is either empty or forms a BIBD, and moreover that when the latter
occurs, any member of the binder of its dual is an oval [2] of this BIBD (Theorem 3.5). The remainder of the paper
concerns the application of this general theory to known infinite families of DTETFs. In particular, in Section 4, we
show that the binder of the dual of any symplectic ETF consists of all affine Lagrangian subspaces of the underlying
finite symplectic space, and compute the parameters of the corresponding BIBD (Theorem 4.5). This unifies some
results of [20, 16, 5] and resolves Conjecture 5.12 of [5] as well as another open problem posed there. We moreover
show that the binder of any symplectic ETF is empty except in a single nontrivial case (Theorem 4.8). In Section 5,
we perform a similar analysis to certain sub-ETFs of symplectic ETFs and their duals that arise from quadratic forms
over the field of two elements [15]. We use techniques from [29, 38] to verify that these ETFs are doubly transitive
(Theorem 5.6). This leads to the new realization that two known infinite families of ETFs have binders that form
BIBDs. These BIBDs are related to the binder of the dual symplectic ETF in nonobvious ways (Theorem 5.9). The
binders of their duals are empty except in a finite number of exceptional cases (Theorems 5.7 and 5.12). A preliminary
version of much of the material of Sections 3 and 4 is given in [33]. See [31] for recent related work.


2. Preliminaries

     We use the phrase “(𝑥 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is a finite sequence in X” as an abbreviation of “𝑛 ↦→ 𝑥 𝑛 is a function from some
finite nonempty set N into some set X.” For any such N and field F we denote the standard basis of the vector space
F N := {𝒙 : N → F} as (𝜹 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N , 𝜹 𝑛 (𝑛′ ) := 1 when 𝑛′ = 𝑛 and 𝜹 𝑛 (𝑛′ ) := 0 when 𝑛′ ≠ 𝑛. Given two such sets M and
N , we identify a linear mapping 𝑨 from F N to F M with a member of F M×N (an “M × N matrix”) in the standard way,
                            Í
namely so that 𝑨(𝜹 𝑛 ) = 𝑚∈ M 𝑨(𝑚, 𝑛)𝜹 𝑚 for all 𝑛 ∈ N . The synthesis map of a finite sequence (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N of vectors
in a vector space V over some field F is 𝜱 : F N → V, 𝜱𝒙 := 𝑛∈ N 𝒙(𝑛)𝝋 𝑛 . Its image is the span of (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N , that
                                                                   Í
is, im(𝜱) = span(𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N . Since ker(𝜱) = {0} if and only if (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is linearly independent, 𝜱 is thus invertible
if and only if (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is a basis for V. Every linear mapping 𝜱 : F N → V is the synthesis map of the unique
finite sequence (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N defined by 𝝋 𝑛 := 𝜱𝜹 𝑛 for each 𝑛 ∈ N . As such, we abuse notation by sometimes denoting
(𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N itself as 𝜱.
     When F is either R or C, we equip F N with the inner product ⟨𝒙 1 , 𝒙 2 ⟩ := 𝑛∈ N 𝒙 1 (𝑛)𝒙 2 (𝑛). Each complex inner
                                                                                   Í
product in this paper is conjugate-linear in its first argument. The adjoint of the synthesis map 𝜱 of a finite sequence
of vectors (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N in a Hilbert space E over F is the analysis map 𝜱 ∗ : E → F N , (𝜱 ∗ 𝒚) (𝑛) = ⟨𝝋 𝑛 , 𝒚⟩. As a special
case of this, we sometimes identify a single vector 𝝋 ∈ E with the mapping 𝝋 : F → E, 𝝋(𝑧) := 𝑧𝝋 whose adjoint is
the linear functional 𝝋∗ : E → F, 𝝋∗ 𝒚 = ⟨𝝋, 𝒚⟩. In general, the corresponding frame operator and Gram matrix are

𝜱𝜱 ∗ : E → E, 𝜱𝜱 ∗ = 𝑛∈ N 𝝋 𝑛 𝝋∗𝑛 and 𝜱 ∗𝜱 ∈ F N×N , (𝜱 ∗𝜱) (𝑛1 , 𝑛2 ) = ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩, respectively. These operators

are positive semidefinite and have rank(𝜱) = dim(span(𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N ) as their rank. Conversely, by the spectral theorem,
any positive semidefinite 𝑮 ∈ F N× N factors as 𝑮 = 𝜱 ∗𝜱 where 𝜱 is the synthesis map of some finite sequence of
vectors (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N that lies in a Hilbert space E over F of dimension rank(𝑮). Such (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N and E are only unique
up to unitary transformations. When E = F M , 𝜱 is the M × N matrix whose 𝑛th column is 𝝋 𝑛 , 𝜱 ∗ is its N × M
conjugate-transpose, and 𝜱𝜱 ∗ and 𝜱 ∗𝜱 are their M × M and N × N products, respectively.
    WeÍsay that (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is a tight frame for E if there exists 𝐴 > 0 such that 𝜱𝜱 ∗ = 𝐴I, namely such that
𝒚 = 𝐴1 𝑛∈ N ⟨𝝋 𝑛 , 𝒚⟩𝝋 𝑛 for all 𝒚 ∈ E. Since this requires (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N to span E, we more generally say that (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N
is a tight frame—without specifying for what space—if it is a tight frame for the subspace im(𝜱) of E that it spans,
namely if there exists 𝐴 > 0 such that 𝜱𝜱 ∗ 𝒚 = 𝐴𝒚 for all 𝒚 ∈ im(𝜱), or equivalently, such that 𝜱𝜱 ∗𝜱 = 𝐴𝜱. As we
now explain, this equates to 𝜱 ∗𝜱 being a positive multiple of a projection (orthogonal projection operator). Indeed,
if 𝜱𝜱 ∗𝜱 = 𝐴𝜱 for some 𝐴 > 0 then 𝑷 := 𝐴1 𝜱 ∗𝜱 satisfies 𝑷∗ 𝑷 = 𝑷. Conversely, if 𝜱 ∗𝜱 = 𝐴𝑷 where 𝐴 > 0 and
𝑷∗ 𝑷 = 𝑷 then since ker( 𝑳 ∗ 𝑳) = ker( 𝑳) in general, ker[𝜱(I − 𝑷)] = ker[(I − 𝑷) 𝐴𝑷(I − 𝑷)] = ker(0) = F N implying
0 = 𝜱(I − 𝑷) = 𝜱 − 𝐴1 𝜱𝜱 ∗𝜱 and so 𝜱𝜱 ∗𝜱 = 𝐴𝜱. Thus, (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is a tight frame if and only if there exists 𝐴 > 0
such that 𝐴𝜱 ∗𝜱 = (𝜱 ∗𝜱) 2 , that is, such that

that is unique up to unitary transformations.

2.1. Equiangular tight frames
    The members of any finite sequence (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N of 𝑁 := #(N ) nonzero vectors in E can be normalized, and this
preserves its spark and coherence (1). A finite sequence (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N of unit norm vectors in E has the (𝐾, 𝛿)-RIP for some
integer 2 ≤ 𝐾 ≤ 𝑁 and real number 0 ≤ 𝛿 < 1 if every 𝐾-vector subsequence (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ K of (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is approximately
orthonormal in the sense that its synthesis map 𝜱 K satisfies ∥𝜱 ∗K 𝜱 K − I∥ 2 ≤ 𝛿, that is, if every eigenvalue of 𝜱 ∗K 𝜱 K
lies in the interval [1 − 𝛿, 1 + 𝛿]. Since 𝛿 < 1 and ker(𝜱 K ) = ker(𝜱 ∗K 𝜱 K ), this is only possible if every 𝐾-vector
subsequence of (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is linearly independent, namely only if spark(𝜱) ≥ 𝐾 + 1. In the special case when 𝐾 = 2,
the optimal (minimal) such 𝛿 is the coherence of (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N :

When 𝑁 > 𝐷, taking a square root of (5) yields the Welch bound (1). Equality holds in it precisely when it holds in

     We more generally say that (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is an ETF—without specifying for what space—if it is both equiangular and
a tight frame. A matrix 𝑮 ∈ F N× N is thus the Gram matrix of an ETF if and only if it is a positive multiple of a
projection (tightness) and its diagonal entries have constant positive value while its off-diagonal entries have constant
positive modulus (equiangularity). Accordingly, an equiangular finite sequence (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is an ETF if and only if (2)
holds for all 𝑛1 , 𝑛3 ∈ N . Here, without loss of generality scaling (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N so that |⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩| = 1 whenever 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2
and ∥𝝋 𝑛 ∥ 2 = 𝑆 for all 𝑛 ∈ NÍ , note that (2) holds when 𝑛1 = 𝑛3 if and only if 𝐴 = 𝑁𝑆−1 + 𝑆, and also that (2) holds when
𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛3 if and only if 𝐴 = 𝑛2 ∈ N ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛2 , 𝝋 𝑛3 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛3 , 𝝋 𝑛1 ⟩. As such, when scaled in this way, an equiangular
finite sequence (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is an ETF if and only if

The summands of (6) are known as triple products. When an ETF (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is scaled in this way, (4) implies that its
dual (𝝍 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is an ETF that enjoys this same scaling, that is, |⟨𝝍 𝑛1 , 𝝍 𝑛2 ⟩| = 1 whenever 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 .

2.2. Equiangular tight frames that contain regular simplices
     Let 𝜱 = (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N be an 𝑁-vector ETF, scaled so that |⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩| = 1 whenever 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 and so we also have
                       −1) 1
∥𝝋 𝑛 ∥ 2 = 𝑆 = [ 𝐷𝑁( 𝑁−𝐷  ] 2 for all 𝑛, where 𝐷 = dim(span(𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N ). Since coh(𝜱) = 𝑆1 , the spark bound (1) gives
spark(𝜱) ≥ 𝑆 +1, namely that any fewer than 𝑆 +1 of these vectors are linearly independent. Any 𝐾-vector subsequence
𝜱 K = (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ K of 𝜱 is equiangular and so is itself an ETF if it happens to be a tight frame. Though seemingly rare,
this remarkable phenomenon nevertheless happens infinitely often in various nontrivial ways [25, 24, 17, 16, 26, 15].
As a special case of this, we say that 𝜱 K is a regular simplex if it is an ETF with rank(𝜱 K ) = 𝐾 − 1. The binder of
an ETF 𝜱 is the set bin(𝜱) of all subsets K of N for which 𝜱 K is a regular simplex.
     Though seemingly hard to compute in general, this binder helps us gauge the ETF’s suitability for compressed
sensing: by Theorem 3.1 of [16], bin(𝜱) is nonempty if and only if spark(𝜱) = 𝑆 + 1, and moreover when this occurs,
K ∈ bin(𝜱) if and only if 𝜱 K is a linearly dependent subsequence of 𝜱 of minimal cardinality 𝐾 = 𝑆 + 1. To explain,
if bin(𝜱) is nonempty then 𝜱 K is a regular simplex for some 𝐾-element subset K of N . Since both 𝜱 and 𝜱 K achieve
equality in the Welch bound, 𝑆1 = coh(𝜱) = coh(𝜱 K ) = 𝐾1−1 and so 𝐾 = 𝑆 + 1. Thus, 𝜱 K is an (𝑆 + 1)-vector ETF for
its 𝑆-dimensional span. When combined with the fact that spark(𝜱) ≥ 𝑆 + 1, this implies that spark(𝜱) = 𝑆 + 1 and
that 𝜱 K is a linearly dependent subsequence of 𝜱 of minimal cardinality 𝐾 = 𝑆 + 1. Conversely, if spark(𝜱) = 𝑆 + 1
then there exists an (𝑆 + 1)-vector linearly dependent subsequence 𝜱 K of 𝜱 that spans a subspace of dimension 𝑆.
Since coh(𝜱 K ) = coh(𝜱) = 𝑆1 , any such 𝜱 K moreover achieves equality in the Welch bound for 𝑆 + 1 vectors in this
subspace, and so is an ETF for this subspace, and thus also a regular simplex. Any such K thus lies in bin(𝜱).
     In particular, 𝜱 achieves equality in both the Welch and spark bounds (1) if and only if it is an ETF that contains
a regular simplex. Such 𝜱 are the “worst of the best” with respect to spark, having the least (worst) spark subject to
the greatest (best) spark bound. From a compressed sensing perspective, it is nevertheless useful to know the binder
                                                                                            𝑁
bin(𝜱) of such an ETF 𝜱 since, for example, the ratio of the cardinality of bin(𝜱) to 𝑆+1      quantifies how frequently
the mapping 𝒙 ↦→ 𝜱𝒙 confuses minimally sparse nonzero signals 𝒙 with 0.
                                                                      4

tight

In particular, bin(𝜱) is a set of certain (𝑆 + 1)-element subsets K of N . As we now explain, objects of this type arise
in combinatorial design, and this has ramifications for the study of ETFs.
    In general, a finite incidence structure is a pair (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) of a finite nonempty (vertex) set V and a finite
sequence (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B of subsets of V, called blocks. In this paper, we use the term “incidence structure” to exclusively
refer to instances of such objects for which, for some integer 𝐾 ≥ 2, we have #(K𝑏 ) = 𝐾 for all 𝑏 ∈ B. Such an
incidence structure is said to be a BIBD if, for some integer Λ, any two distinct vertices are contained in exactly Λ
blocks. For any 𝑣 ∈ V, counting {(𝑣 ′ , 𝑏) ∈ V × B : 𝑣, 𝑣 ′ ∈ K𝑏 , 𝑣 ′ ≠ 𝑣} in two ways gives that the number 𝑅 of blocks
that contain 𝑣 satisfies (𝑉 − 1)Λ = 𝑅(𝐾 − 1). This implies that Λ ≥ 1 and also that 𝑅 is independent of 𝑣. Similarly,
𝐵 = #(B) satisfies 𝐵𝐾 = #{(𝑣, 𝑏) ∈ V × B : 𝑣 ∈ K𝑏 } = 𝑉 𝑅. The incidence matrix of (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) is 𝑿 ∈ R B×V
where 𝑿 (𝑏, 𝑣) := 1 if 𝑣 ∈ K𝑏 and 𝑿 (𝑏, 𝑣) := 0 otherwise. Letting 1 V , 1 B and 1 V ×V be the appropriate all-ones
vectors and matrix, respectively, 𝑿 satisfies

    In general, we say that a finite sequence (𝝍 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N of vectors in a Hilbert space is equivalent to another such sequence
(𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N if there exists a finite sequence (𝑧 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N of unimodular scalars such that ⟨𝝍 𝑛1 , 𝝍 𝑛2 ⟩ = 𝑧 𝑛1 𝑧 𝑛2 ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩ for
all 𝑛1 , 𝑛2 ∈ N , namely such that 𝝍 𝑛 = 𝑧 𝑛𝑼𝝋 𝑛 for all 𝑛, where 𝑼 is some unitary transformation from the span of
(𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N onto that of (𝝍 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N . Any cycle of inner products is preserved by this notion of equivalence, including
∥𝝋 𝑛 ∥ 2 = ⟨𝝋 𝑛 , 𝝋 𝑛 ⟩ for any 𝑛, |⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩| 2 = ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛2 , 𝝋 𝑛1 ⟩ for any 𝑛1 and 𝑛2 , and triple products:

                   ⟨𝑧 𝑛1 𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝑧 𝑛2 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩⟨𝑧 𝑛2 𝝋 𝑛2 , 𝑧 𝑛3 𝝋 𝑛3 ⟩⟨𝑧 𝑛3 𝝋 𝑛3 , 𝑧 𝑛1 𝝋 𝑛1 ⟩ = ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛2 , 𝝋 𝑛3 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛3 , 𝝋 𝑛1 ⟩.   (11)

In particular, by (6) and (7), any finite sequence of vectors that is equivalent to an ETF (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is itself an ETF
and the two ETFs have the same binder. This notion of equivalence moreover preserves tightness (2), duals, the RIP,
coherence, and spark. We caution however that it does not preserve certain other properties of note such as centroidal
symmetry [19] and flatness [18].
                                                                             5
3. The binders of doubly transitive equiangular tight frames

     As detailed above, the binder of an ETF 𝜱 = (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N is the set bin(𝜱) of all subsets K of N for which
                                                                                                                      −1) 1
𝜱 K = (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ K is a regular simplex. Many ETFs have empty binders [16]. Indeed by (7), this occurs if 𝑆 := [ 𝐷𝑁( 𝑁−𝐷  ]2
is not an integer, or if ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩ is an odd root of unity for any distinct 𝑛1 , 𝑛2 ∈ N . Other ETFs, including some
harmonic ETFs, have binders that seemingly consist of a single partition of N [16, 26]. Experimentation reveals that
yet other ETFs have nontrivial binders with little discernible combinatorial structure. That said, a remarkable number
of ETFs 𝜱 have binders that are rich enough for there to exist a BIBD of the form (N , (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) where K𝑏 ∈ bin(𝜱) for
all 𝑏 ∈ B. As reviewed above, this in turn permits a (sparse but high-dimensional) phased incidence representation (9)
of its dual 𝜳 [16]. Infinite families of such ETFs are known for example, including—via Theorem 5.2 of [16]—the
phased BIBD ETFs of Theorems 5.1 and 5.2 of [20], and the Gabor–Steiner ETFs of Theorems 5.6 and 5.8 of [5]. In
fact, these results from [5] show that certain ETFs have the following stronger property:
Definition 3.1. We say the binder bin(𝜱) of an ETF 𝜱 forms a BIBD if (N , (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) is a BIBD for some enumeration
(K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B of bin(𝜱), i.e., for some bijection 𝑏 ↦→ K𝑏 from B onto bin(𝜱).
    In particular, when the binder of ETF 𝜱 forms a BIBD we can represent its dual 𝜳 as a phased incidence
matrix (10) that has no repeated blocks, nor any “missing” blocks. Numerical experimentation provides a number
of small-parameter ETFs with this property [16] that are not explained by the results of [5]. In this section, we
exploit the recently introduced concept of a doubly transitive equiangular tight frame (DTETF) to better explain this
phenomenon. Many ETFs are DTETFs [27, 28, 12], and we show that the binder of any DTETF is either empty or
forms a BIBD. In the next section, we combine this result with a key proof technique of [5] to resolve some problems
posed there, showing that the binder of the dual of any symplectic ETF forms a BIBD that consists of all affine
Lagrangian subspaces. In Section 5, we use a similar approach to calculate the binder of any member of two other
infinite families of DTETFs and their duals.
    In general, a group G is a permutation group of a finite set N if it is a subgroup of the symmetric group Sym(N )
of all permutations on N . For any such G, the mapping (𝜎, 𝑛) ↦→ 𝜎(𝑛) is its natural action on N . Such an action
is doubly transitive if, for any 𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 , 𝑛4 ∈ N with 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 and 𝑛3 ≠ 𝑛4 , there exists 𝜎 ∈ G such that 𝜎(𝑛1 ) = 𝑛3
and 𝜎(𝑛2 ) = 𝑛4 . Any such action is necessarily transitive, that is, for any 𝑛1 , 𝑛2 ∈ N there exists 𝜎 ∈ G such that
𝜎(𝑛1 ) = 𝑛2 . We consider how such actions arise in the classical theory of BIBDs as well as their connection to ETFs.
    Let 𝑉 ≥ 𝐾 ≥ 2, and let (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) be an incidence structure with #(V) = 𝑉 and #(K𝑏 ) = 𝐾 for all 𝑏 ∈ B. A
subset L of V is an arc of (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) if #(K𝑏 ∩ L) ≤ 2 for all 𝑏 ∈ B. A permutation 𝜎 of V is an automorphism
of (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) if there exists a permutation 𝜏 of B such that, for any 𝑣 ∈ V and 𝑏 ∈ B, 𝑣 ∈ K𝑏 if and only if
𝜎(𝑣) ∈ K 𝜏 (𝑏) . The corresponding automorphism group is the set Aut(V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) of all such 𝜎, which is a subgroup
of Sym(V). If 𝜎 ∈ Aut(V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) then 𝜎(K𝑏 ) := {𝜎(𝑣) : 𝑣 ∈ K𝑏 } = {𝜎(𝑣) : 𝜎(𝑣) ∈ K 𝜏 (𝑏) } = K 𝜏 (𝑏) for all
𝑏 ∈ B. In particular, any such 𝜎 maps any block to another. The converse is true if we further assume that (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B
contains no repeated blocks, that is, if K𝑏1 ≠ K𝑏2 when 𝑏 1 ≠ 𝑏 2 : in that case, if 𝜎 is any permutation of V that maps
any block to another, then for each 𝑏 ∈ B, there exists a unique 𝜏(𝑏) ∈ B such that 𝜎(K𝑏 ) = K 𝜏 (𝑏) , and the mapping
𝑏 ↦→ 𝜏(𝑏) is moreover a permutation of B since if 𝜏(𝑏 1 ) = 𝜏(𝑏 2 ) then K𝑏1 = 𝜎 −1 (K 𝜏 (𝑏1 ) ) = 𝜎 −1 (K 𝜏 (𝑏2 ) ) = K𝑏2
and so 𝑏 1 = 𝑏 2 . In general, we say that the incidence structure (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) is “doubly transitive” when the natural
action of Aut(V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) on V is doubly transitive. The next result combines combinatorial design folklore with
some results about arcs from [2]. For the sake of completeness, a proof of it is given in the appendix.
Lemma 3.2 ([2]). Let 𝑉 ≥ 𝐾 ≥ 2 be integers and let (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B be a finite sequence of 𝐾-element subsets of the
𝑉-element set V. Then:
  (a) If (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) is doubly transitive then it is a BIBD.
                                                                                  𝑉 −1
  (b) If (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) is a BIBD, then any 𝐿-element arc L of it satisfies 𝐿 ≤ 𝐾 −1 + 1.
                    𝑉 −1
      Moreover, 𝐿 = 𝐾 −1 + 1 if and only if L is nonempty and #(K𝑏 ∩ L) ∈ {0, 2} for all 𝑏 ∈ B.
      When this occurs, we say that L is an oval of (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ).
  (c) If (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) is doubly transitive and has an oval then letting (L 𝑎 ) 𝑎∈ A be an enumeration of its ovals,
      (V, (L 𝑎 ) 𝑎∈ A ) is itself a doubly transitive BIBD and has each K𝑏 as an oval.

the binder of its dual 𝜳 is an oval of this BIBD.

K ∈ bin(𝜱), or equivalently, that if {𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 } ⊆ K ∈ bin(𝜱) and 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 ≠ 𝑛3 ≠ 𝑛1 then {𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 } ⊈ L. Here,
note that by (7), having the former gives ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛2 , 𝝋 𝑛3 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛3 , 𝝋 𝑛1 ⟩ = −1. But since ⟨𝝍 𝑛1 , 𝝍 𝑛2 ⟩ = −⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩
whenever 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 , this implies that ⟨𝝍 𝑛1 , 𝝍 𝑛2 ⟩⟨𝝍 𝑛2 , 𝝍 𝑛3 ⟩⟨𝝍 𝑛3 , 𝝍 𝑛1 ⟩ = −(−1) 3 = 1 ≠ −1, implying via (7) that indeed
{𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 } ⊈ L.                                                                                                               □
     One nonobvious consequence of Lemma 3.2 and Theorem 3.3 is that, under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.3, no
member of bin(𝜳 ) can intersect any member of bin(𝜱) in exactly one index 𝑛. In the special case where (N , (L 𝑎 ) 𝑎∈ A )
is itself a BIBD where L 𝑎 ∈ bin(𝜳 ) for all 𝑎 ∈ A, there is an alternative way to observe this: since 𝜳 ∗𝜳 = 𝐴I − 𝜱 ∗𝜱
where (𝜱 ∗𝜱) 2 = 𝐴𝜱 ∗𝜱 we have ∥𝜳𝜱 ∗ ∥ 2Fro = Tr(𝜱𝜳 ∗𝜳𝜱 ∗ ) = Tr(𝜱 ∗𝜱( 𝐴I − 𝜱 ∗𝜱)) = Tr(0) = 0, and so 𝜳𝜱 ∗ = 0.
As such, the rows of the particular representation of 𝜳 given in (10) are necessarily orthogonal to the rows of the
analogous representation of 𝜱. Such rows are supported on K ∈ bin(𝜱) and L ∈ bin(𝜳 ), respectively, and so cannot
be orthogonal if #(K ∩ L) = 1.
     In order to relate these concepts to DTETFs we now review some ideas from [27, 28]. Here let 𝜱 = (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N
be an equal-norm finite sequence of vectors in a Hilbert space E. A symmetry of 𝜱 is a permutation 𝜎 of N for
which (𝝋 𝜎 (𝑛) ) 𝑛∈ N is equivalent to (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N , that is, for which there exist unimodular scalars (𝑧 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N such that
⟨𝝋 𝜎 (𝑛1 ) , 𝝋 𝜎 (𝑛2 ) ⟩ = 𝑧 𝑛1 𝑧 𝑛2 ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩ whenever 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 . It is straightforward to verify that the set Sym(𝜱) of all
such symmetries 𝜎 is a subgroup of Sym(N ). If 𝜱 is an equal norm tight frame with dim(span(𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N ) < #(N )
then its dual 𝜳 is also an equal norm tight frame, and (4) immediately implies that Sym(𝜱) = Sym(𝜳 ). Though we
refer to Sym(𝜱) as the symmetry group of 𝜱, it is more accurate to call it the symmetry group of the corresponding
finite sequence of lines (⟨𝝋 𝑛 ⟩) 𝑛∈ N , where ⟨𝝋 𝑛 ⟩ := {𝑧𝝋 𝑛 : 𝑧 ∈ F} is the one-dimensional subspace of E that contains
any particular vector 𝝋 𝑛 . Indeed, though not necessary for our work here, it is straightforward to verify [27] that
𝜎 ∈ Sym(𝜱) if and only if there exists a unitary operator 𝑼 on E such that ⟨𝝋 𝜎 (𝑛) ⟩ = 𝑼⟨𝝋 𝑛 ⟩ for each 𝑛. We now
consider a streamlined version of a result from [27] that relates ETFs to doubly transitive actions. Our proof of it relies
on triple products, and is given in the appendix.

Lemma 3.4 (Lemma 1.1 of [27]). Let 𝜱 = (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N be an equal norm finite sequence of 𝑁 vectors in a Hilbert space.
Then if dim(span(𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N ) < 𝑁 and the natural action of its symmetry group Sym(𝜱) on N is doubly transitive then
𝜱 is an ETF. We call any such 𝜱 a DTETF.
    The dual 𝜳 of any DTETF 𝜱 has Sym(𝜳 ) = Sym(𝜱) and so is itself a DTETF. With these ideas in hand, we now
state and prove our most fundamental result:

Theorem 3.5. If 𝜱 is a DTETF then its binder is either empty or forms a BIBD, and in the latter case, any member of
the binder of its dual 𝜳 is an oval of this BIBD.
Proof. Let 𝜱 = (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N be an ETF, scaled without loss of generality so that |⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩| = 1 when 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 . Let
TP(𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 ) := ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛2 , 𝝋 𝑛3 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛3 , 𝝋 𝑛1 ⟩ be the triple product that corresponds to a given 𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 ∈ N .

                                                                 7
For any 𝜎 ∈ Sym(𝜱), there exists a finite sequence of unimodular scalars (𝑧 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N such that ⟨𝝋 𝜎 (𝑛1 ) , 𝝋 𝜎 (𝑛2 ) ⟩ =
𝑧 𝑛1 𝑧 𝑛2 ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩ when 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 . For any 𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 ∈ N , this implies via (11) that TP(𝜎(𝑛1 ), 𝜎(𝑛2 ), 𝜎(𝑛3 )) =
TP(𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 ). In particular, if K is a subset of N of cardinality 𝑆 + 1 and TP(𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 ) = −1 for all pairwise distinct
𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 ∈ K then 𝜎(K) := {𝜎(𝑛) : 𝑛 ∈ K} has these same properties. By (7), this implies that any symmetry
𝜎 of 𝜱 maps any member K of the binder of 𝜱 to another such member. That is, if bin(𝜱) is nonempty then
Sym(𝜱) ≤ Aut(N , (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) where (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B is any enumeration of bin(𝜱).
      As such, if 𝜱 is a DTETF with a nonempty binder then Sym(𝜱) ≤ Aut(N , (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) where the natural action of
Sym(𝜱) on N is doubly transitive, implying that the same is true for Aut(N , (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ). By Lemma 3.2, (N , (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B )
is a BIBD with 𝑉 = 𝑁 and 𝐾 = 𝑆 + 1. By Theorem 3.3, any member of the binder of the dual 𝜳 of 𝜱 is an oval of
this BIBD. Here in fact, 𝜳 is itself a DTETF since Sym(𝜳 ) = Sym(𝜱), and so when its binder is also nonempty, any
member of either of the two resulting BIBDs is an oval of the other.                                                           □
    In Theorem II.6 of [30], a similar triple-product-based argument is used to show that every triply transitive ETF is
either a regular simplex or an ETF for a space of dimension one. In fact, such ETFs are totally symmetric. See also
Lemma 6.2 of [28]. Theorem 3.5 still applies in such cases, but the resulting BIBDs are degenerate:
Example 3.6. For any positive integer 𝑆, let 𝜱 = (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N be an (𝑆 + 1)-vector regular simplex, namely an ETF
whose span has dimension 𝑆. Its dual 𝜳 is thus an (𝑆 + 1)-vector ETF for a space of dimension one. Without loss
of generality scaling 𝜱 and 𝜳 so that |⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩| = |⟨𝝍 𝑛1 , 𝝍 𝑛2 ⟩| = 1 when 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 , any such ETF 𝜳 consists, up
to equivalence, of 𝑆 + 1 copies of the scalar 1. Choosing 𝜳 in this way and modifying its dual 𝜱 accordingly gives
𝜱 ∗𝜱 = (𝑆 + 1)I − 𝜳 ∗𝜳 = (𝑆 + 1)I − 1 N× N , that is, ∥𝝋 𝑛 ∥ 2 = 𝑆 for all 𝑛 and ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩ = −1 when 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 . The
synthesis map of such a 𝜱 can be constructed, for example, by taking a possibly-complex Hadamard matrix of size
𝑆 + 1, scaling its columns as necessary so that it has an all-ones row, and then removing that row.
     Clearly, every permutation 𝜎 of N is a symmetry of 𝜳 , and so Sym(𝜱) = Sym(𝜳 ) = Sym(N ). Since the natural
action of Sym(N ) on N is doubly transitive—in fact it is totally transitive—this implies that 𝜱 and 𝜳 are DTETFs.
As such, Theorem 3.5 applies to them. The binder of the regular simplex 𝜱 consists of the single set N . Indeed since
⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛2 , 𝝋 𝑛3 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛3 , 𝝋 𝑛1 ⟩ = (−1) 3 = −1 for any 𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 ∈ N with 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 ≠ 𝑛3 ≠ 𝑛1 , (7) gives that a subset K
of N belongs to bin(𝜱) if and only if #(K) = 𝑆 + 1, namely if and only if K = N . Since bin(𝜱) = {N } is nonempty,
Theorem 3.5 guarantees that it forms a BIBD. In fact, it is a degenerate BIBD with 𝑉 = 𝐾 = 𝑆 + 1 and Λ = 1 whose
B × N incidence matrix 𝑿 consists of a single all-ones row. Since ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩ = −1 when 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 , we can further
choose 𝑧 𝑏,𝑛 = 1 for all 𝑏 and 𝑛 when representing its dual 𝜳 as in (10), yielding an “all-ones row vector.”
     In contrast, the binder of its dual 𝜳 consists of all two-element subsets of N : when “(𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N ” is (𝝍 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N , “𝑁”,
“𝐷” and “𝑆” become 𝑆 + 1, 1 and 1, respectively, and so (7) gives that a subset K of N belongs to bin(𝜳 ) if and
only if #(K) = 2 and −1 = ⟨𝝍 𝑛1 , 𝝍 𝑛2 ⟩⟨𝝍 𝑛2 , 𝝍 𝑛3 ⟩⟨𝝍 𝑛3 , 𝝍 𝑛1 ⟩ = 1 for all 𝑛1 , 𝑛2 , 𝑛3 ∈ K with 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 ≠ 𝑛3 ≠ 𝑛1 ,
and though this second property is impossible when #(K) ≥ 3, it is vacuously true when #(K) = 2. Since
bin(𝜳 ) = {𝑛1 , 𝑛2 } : 𝑛1 , 𝑛2 ∈ N , 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛2 is nonempty, Theorem 3.5 guarantees that it forms a BIBD. In fact, it is

one nonzero entry in common. As guaranteed by Theorem 3.5, every member of bin(𝜳 ) is an oval of bin(𝜱), and
vice versa. Here in fact, the sole member of bin(𝜱) intersects every member of bin(𝜳 ) in exactly two vertices.

its dual 𝜳 —from a standard Hadamard matrix of size 4:

                                                                 8
4. The binders of symplectic equiangular tight frames and their duals

     In the previous section, we discussed when a finite sequence 𝜱 = (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N of vectors in a Hilbert space is a
DTETF (Lemma 3.4), and showed that the binder of any such 𝜱 is either empty or forms a BIBD (Theorem 3.5). To
date, we have not found a general method for determining when the binder of a DTETF is nonempty, nor if so, for
computing the “Λ” parameter of the resulting BIBD. That said, [28, 12] provides a complete classification of DTETFs,
and analyzing each class of them individually seems like a surmountable task for the community. In support of such
a program, we devote this section and the next to performing such analyses for specific classes of DTETFs that arise
from symplectic and quadratic forms on finite vector spaces, respectively.
     As elegantly summarized in Example 5.10 of [27], symplectic ETFs have been independently discovered several
times, and—up to equivalence—in several guises. Some facts about the binders of their duals are already known:
combining Theorem 5.1 of [20] and Theorem 5.2 of [16] gives that any such binder contains a (Desarguesian) affine
plane. Theorem 5.6 of [5] gives that it is in fact an affine plane when the underlying finite vector space has dimension 2.
When this space instead has dimension 4, Theorem 5.8 of [5] gives that it forms a more exotic BIBD. Conjecture 5.12
of [5] moreover predicts that the binder of the dual of any symplectic ETF forms a BIBD with a particular Λ parameter.
The approach of [5] relied on their novel use of the triple-product-based characterization of regular simplices (7) as a
symbolic tool, rather than simply a numerical aid [16]. In this section, we combine that trick with both Theorem 3.5
and a clean expression for the Gram matrix of the symplectic ETF to prove that Conjecture 5.12 of [5] is partially
true—the binder of its dual does indeed form a BIBD—but is also partially false, as its prediction for the value of Λ
is incorrect in general. We in fact show that, in general, this binder consists of all affine Lagrangian subspaces of the
underlying finite symplectic space. We begin here by reviewing symplectic ETFs.

4.1. Symplectic forms
    A function B : V × V → F is a bilinear form on a vector space V if, for any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V, the mappings
𝒗 ↦→ B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗) and 𝒗 ↦→ B(𝒗, 𝒗 2 ) are linear. Such a function B is symmetric if B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = B(𝒗 2 , 𝒗 1 ) for all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V,
and is alternating if B(𝒗, 𝒗) = 0 for all 𝒗 ∈ V. For any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V, the latter implies 0 = B(𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) =
B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) + B(𝒗 2 , 𝒗 1 ), that is, B(𝒗 2 , 𝒗 1 ) = −B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ). A symplectic form on V is an alternating bilinear form B that
is nondegenerate in the sense that for any 𝒗 1 ≠ 0 there exists 𝒗 2 ∈ V such that B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) ≠ 0. When this occurs, we
can scale such a vector 𝒗 2 so as to assume without loss of generality that B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1.
    For the remainder of this section, let B be a symplectic form on a finite-dimensional vector space V over some field
F. Vectors 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V are orthogonal if B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 0. Since B is alternating, this occurs if and only if B(𝒗 2 , 𝒗 1 ) = 0,
and so we need not distinguish left- and right-orthogonality. The orthogonal complement of any subset S of V is
S ⊥ := {𝒗 2 ∈ V : B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 0, ∀ 𝒗 1 ∈ S}. The algebraic properties of symplectic forms differ from those of real
inner products in some key respects: for example, since every vector is orthogonal to itself, orthogonality does not
guarantee linear independence, and orthonormal bases do not exist. That said, some nontrivial properties of real inner
products generalize to the symplectic setting. We now briefly review those that we need here.
    For example, we claim that dim(V0 ) + dim(V0⊥ ) = dim(V) for any subspace V0 of V. To see this, extend a basis
       𝑀0
(𝒗 𝑚 ) 𝑚=1   for V0 to a basis (𝒗 𝑚 ) 𝑚=1𝑀 for V. The nondegeneracy of B implies that 𝑳 : V → F 𝑀 , ( 𝑳𝒗) (𝑚) := B(𝒗 , 𝒗)
                                                                                                                                  𝑚
has ker( 𝑳) = {0}. By the rank-nullity theorem, 𝑳 is invertible. Composing 𝑳 with the truncated identity matrix
𝑨 ∈ F 𝑀0 × 𝑀 , 𝑨(𝑚 1 , 𝑚 2 ) := 𝜹 𝑚2 (𝑚 1 ) yields 𝑨𝑳 : V → F 𝑀0 , ( 𝑨𝑳) (𝑚) = B(𝒗 𝑚 , 𝒗) for all 𝑚 ∈ [𝑀0 ]. Thus,
dim(V) − dim(V0⊥ ) = dim(V) − dim(ker( 𝑨𝑳)) = rank( 𝑨𝑳) = rank( 𝑨) = 𝑀0 = dim(V0 ), giving the claim. This
in turn implies that (S ⊥ ) ⊥ = span(S) for any subset S of V: letting V0 = span(S), we have V0⊥ = S ⊥ where clearly
V0 ⊆ (V0⊥ ) ⊥ and dim(V0 ) = dim(V) − dim(V0⊥ ) = dim((V0⊥ ) ⊥ ). We moreover claim that any linear functional
𝑳 : V → F has a Riesz representation, that is, there exists 𝒗 𝑳 ∈ V such that 𝑳(𝒗) = B(𝒗 𝑳 , 𝒗) for all 𝒗 ∈ V. Indeed,
take 𝒗 𝑳 = 0 when 𝑳 = 0. When instead 𝑳 ≠ 0, ker( 𝑳) ⊥ has dimension one and so contains some 𝒗 1 ≠ 0. Take 𝒗 2
such that B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1 and so 𝒗 2 ∉ V0 . For any 𝒗 ∈ V, there thus exists 𝑐 ∈ F and 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 such that 𝒗 = 𝑐𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 .
Here, B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗) = 𝑐1 + 0 = 𝑐 and so 𝑳𝒗 = 𝑐𝑳𝒗 2 + 0 = B(𝒗 𝑳 , 𝒗) where 𝒗 𝑳 := ( 𝑳𝒗 2 )𝒗 1 . Having the claim, we moreover
note that since B is nondegenerate, this 𝒗 𝑳 is unique.
    A pair (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) of vectors in V is symplectic if B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1. Such a pair exists if and only if V ≠ {0}. In fact,
as noted above, for any nonzero 𝒗 1 ∈ V there exists 𝒗 2 ∈ V such that (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) is symplectic. For any symplectic
pair (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ), span{𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 } has dimension 2 since otherwise 𝒗 1 and 𝒗 2 are collinear and B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 0, a contradiction.
Thus, V0 := {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }⊥ has dim(V0 ) = 𝑀 − 2 where 𝑀 = dim(V). We moreover claim that any 𝒗 ∈ V can be uniquely
                                                                  9
written as 𝒗 = 𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥 2 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 where 𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ∈ F and 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 . Indeed, taking the B-form of this equation with 𝒗 1
and 𝒗 2 implies that necessarily 𝑥1 = B(𝒗, 𝒗 2 ) and 𝑥2 = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗), and so 𝒗 0 is necessarily 𝒗 − 𝑥1 𝒗 1 − 𝑥 2 𝒗 2 . Conversely,
choosing 𝑥1 , 𝑥 2 and 𝒗 0 in this way gives 𝒗 = 𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 where 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 , yielding the claim. In particular,
V0 ∩ V0⊥ = {0} and V is the internal direct sum of V0 and V0⊥ = span{𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }. The restriction B0 : V0 × V0 → F of
B to V0 is itself symplectic: it is immediately bilinear and alternating, and is nondegenerate since if 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 satisfies
B(𝒗 0 , 𝒗) = 0 for all 𝒗 ∈ V0 then 𝒗 ∈ V0⊥ , implying 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 ∩ V0⊥ = {0}, and so 𝒗 0 = 0. Provided V0 ≠ {0}, this
allows one to repeat this procedure with “V” being V0 , that is, to choose a symplectic pair (𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ) from V0 , which is
a space of dimension 𝑀0 = 𝑀 − 2. Since we have assumed here that the dimension 𝑀 of V is finite, proceeding in
this manner by induction reveals that 𝑀 is necessarily even—since no alternating bilinear form on a one-dimensional

4.2. Symplectic equiangular tight frames
     As we now explain, in the special case where F is the finite field F 𝑃 = {0, . . . , 𝑃 − 1} of prime order 𝑃, the
symplectic form B on a vector space V of dimension 2𝐽 over F 𝑃 gives rise to a DTETF whose vectors are indexed
by V, which has order 𝑁 = 𝑃2𝐽 . When B is more generally a symplectic form on a finite-dimensional vector space
V over a finite field F𝑄 whose order 𝑄 is a power of this prime 𝑃, we claim that the requisite F 𝑃 -valued form can
be obtained by composing B with any nontrivial linear functional 𝑳 : F𝑄 → F 𝑃 . Indeed, it is clear that V is a
finite-dimensional space over F 𝑃 whose dimension is the product of that of V over F𝑄 with that of F𝑄 over F 𝑃 ,
and moreover that 𝑳 ◦ B : V × V → F 𝑃 is an alternating bilinear form. To see that 𝑳 ◦ B is nondegenerate, for
any nonzero 𝒗 1 ∈ V, take 𝒗 2 ∈ V such that B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1 and any 𝑥 ∈ F𝑄 such that 𝑳(𝑥) ≠ 0, and note that
( 𝑳 ◦ B) (𝒗 1 , 𝑥𝒗 2 ) = 𝑳(B(𝒗 1 , 𝑥𝒗 2 )) = 𝑳(𝑥B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 )) =Í𝑳(𝑥) ≠ 0, yielding the claim. For example, one can always
                                                                    𝐼 −1 𝑃 𝑖
choose 𝑳 to be the field trace, that is, 𝑳(𝑥) = tr(𝑥) := 𝑖=0             𝑥 where 𝑄 = 𝑃 𝐼 .
     The character table of an F 𝑃 -valued symplectic form B is the matrix 𝜞 ∈ F V ×V , 𝜞(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) := exp( 2𝑃𝜋i B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 )).
Here, F = C unless 𝑃 = 2 and we can choose F = R. It can be shown that 𝜞 is the classical character table of the (finite
abelian) additive group of V with respect to a particular enumeration of its Pontryagin dual. We claim that 𝜞 is a
possibly-complex Hadamard matrix. Indeed, (𝜞𝜞 ∗ ) (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 𝒗 ∈ V 𝜞(𝒗 1 , 𝒗)𝜞(𝒗 2 , 𝒗) = 𝒗 ∈ V exp( 2𝑃𝜋i B(𝒗 1 − 𝒗 2 , 𝒗))

an ETF 𝜱 and its dual 𝜳 , respectively. We refer to these ETFs as follows:
Definition 4.1. Let 𝑃 be prime, B be a symplectic form on a vector space V over F 𝑃 of dimension 2𝐽, and 𝜞 ∈ F V ×V ,

and −𝑃 𝐽 = −4 with multiplicities 𝐷 = 12 𝑃 𝐽 (𝑃 𝐽 + 1) = 21 22 (22 + 1) = 10 and 𝑁 − 𝐷 = 12 𝑃 𝐽 (𝑃 𝐽 − 1) = 12 22 (22 − 1) = 6,
respectively, implying 4I + 𝜞 2 and 4I − 𝜞 2 are Gram matrices of 16-vector ETFs for spaces of dimension 10 and 6,
respectively. Similarly, 8I + 𝜞 3 and 8I − 𝜞 3 are Gram matrices of 64-vector ETFs for spaces of dimension 36 and 28,
respectively, where 𝜞 3 = 𝜞 1 ⊗ 𝜞 1 ⊗ 𝜞 1 . Later on in this section, we calculate the binders of all such ETFs.
                                                                             11
     Any symplectic ETF 𝜱 and its dual 𝜳 are DTETFs [27]. To explain, in general, if B is any symplectic form on a
finite-dimensional vector space V over some field F, the corresponding symplectic group is the set Sp(B) of all linear
operators 𝑨 : V → V that preserve B, that is, for which B( 𝑨𝒗 1 , 𝑨𝒗 2 ) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) for all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V. In light of (12),
any such 𝑨 maps any symplectic basis of V to another such basis. Since any such V has a symplectic basis, and since
any symplectic basis for V is a basis for it, any such 𝑨 is necessarily invertible. Thus, Sp(B) is necessarily a subset
of the general linear group GL(V) of all invertible linear operators on V. It is moreover straightforward to show that
it is a subgroup of GL(V). Thus,

                        Sp(B) = { 𝑨 ∈ GL(V) : B( 𝑨𝒗 1 , 𝑨𝒗 2 ) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ), ∀ 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V} ≤ GL(V).                                 (15)
                                                                                                                    2𝐽
Conversely, any linear operator 𝑨 : V → V that maps even a single symplectic basis (𝒗 𝑚 ) 𝑚=1                           for V to another such
                                                                                                                                     2𝐽
basis is necessarily a member of Sp(B) since any 𝒖 1 , 𝒖 2 ∈ V can be written as linear combinations of (𝒗 𝑚 ) 𝑚=1                        where
B( 𝑨𝒗 𝑚1 , 𝑨𝒗 𝑚2 ) = B(𝒗 𝑚1 , 𝒗 𝑚2 ) for all 𝑚 1 , 𝑚 2 ∈ M. From this, it follows that the natural action of Sp(B) on V\{0}
is transitive, that is, that for any nonzero 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V, there exists 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B) such that 𝑨𝒗 1 = 𝒗 2 : since any nonzero
vector can serve as the first member of a symplectic basis for V, extending 𝒗 1 to one such basis and 𝒗 2 to another, the
unique linear operator 𝑨 : V → V that maps the former onto the latter thus belongs to Sp(B).
      We now more broadly consider affine symplectic mappings, that is, bijections from V onto itself of the form
𝒗 ′ ↦→ (𝒗, 𝑨) · 𝒗 ′ := 𝒗 + 𝑨𝒗 ′ where 𝒗 ∈ V and 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B). Composing two such mappings yields another:
(𝒗 1 , 𝑨1 )· [(𝒗 2 , 𝑨2 )·𝒗 ′ ] = 𝒗 1 + 𝑨1 (𝒗 2 + 𝑨2 𝒗 ′ ) = (𝒗 1 + 𝑨1 𝒗 2 , 𝑨1 𝑨2 )·𝒗 ′ . It quickly follows that (𝒗, 𝑨)·𝒗 ′ := 𝒗+ 𝑨𝒗 ′ defines
an action on V by the semidirect product V ⋊Sp(B) under the group operation (𝒗 1 , 𝑨1 ) (𝒗 2 , 𝑨2 ) := (𝒗 1 + 𝑨1 𝒗 2 , 𝑨1 𝑨2 ).
This action is doubly transitive since, for any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ∈ V with 𝒗 1 ≠ 𝒗 2 and 𝒗 3 ≠ 𝒗 4 , taking 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B) such that
𝑨(𝒗 1 − 𝒗 2 ) = (𝒗 3 − 𝒗 4 ) and 𝒗 = 𝒗 3 − 𝑨𝒗 1 , we have (𝒗, 𝑨) · 𝒗 1 = 𝒗 + 𝑨𝒗 1 = 𝒗 3 and

                     (𝒗, 𝑨) · 𝒗 2 = 𝒗 + 𝑨𝒗 2 = 𝒗 3 − 𝑨𝒗 1 + 𝑨𝒗 2 = 𝒗 3 − 𝑨(𝒗 1 − 𝒗 2 ) = 𝒗 3 − (𝒗 3 − 𝒗 4 ) = 𝒗 4 .

Such mappings have a simple effect on symplectic forms: for any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V,

                                 B(𝒗 + 𝑨𝒗 1 , 𝒗 + 𝑨𝒗 2 ) = B( 𝑨𝒗 1 , 𝒗) + B(𝒗, 𝑨𝒗 2 ) + B( 𝑨𝒗 1 , 𝑨𝒗 2 )
                                                            = −B(𝒗, 𝑨𝒗 1 ) + B(𝒗, 𝑨𝒗 2 ) + B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ).

When regarded as permutations of V, these mappings thus belong to the symmetry group of the symplectic ETF
(Definition 4.1): for any 𝒗 ∈ V and 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B), (𝑧 𝒗 ′ )𝒗 ′ ∈ V , 𝑧 𝒗 ′ := exp( 2𝑃𝜋i B(𝒗, 𝑨𝒗 ′ )) is a finite sequence of unimodular
scalars, and the following holds for any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V, 𝒗 1 ≠ 𝒗 2 :

                              ⟨𝝋𝒗+𝑨𝒗1 , 𝝋𝒗+𝑨𝒗2 ⟩ = exp( 2𝑃𝜋i B(𝒗 + 𝑨𝒗 1 , 𝒗 + 𝑨𝒗 2 ))
                                                    = exp( 2𝑃𝜋i [−B(𝒗, 𝑨𝒗 1 ) + B(𝒗, 𝑨𝒗 2 ) + B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 )])
                                                    = 𝑧 𝒗1 𝑧 𝒗2 ⟨𝝋𝒗1 , 𝝋𝒗2 ⟩.

One may more strongly show that these affine symplectic mappings are the only members of the symmetry group of a
symplectic ETF 𝜱, that is, 𝜱  V ⋊ Sp(B) [27]. Regardless, by the argument above, any symplectic ETF is a DTETF,
and so its dual is as well. By Theorem 3.5, the binders of these DTETFs are thus either empty or form BIBDs. We
now calculate these binders explicitly.

4.3. Calculating the binders of symplectic equiangular tight frames and their duals
    The process of calculating the binder of a symplectic ETF has much in common with that for its dual:
Lemma 4.3. For any prime 𝑃 and positive integer 𝐽, let bin(𝜱) be the binder of a 𝑃2𝐽 -vector symplectic ETF 𝜱, and
bin(𝜳 ) be the binder of its dual 𝜳 , as given in Definition 4.1. Then:
  (a) bin(𝜱) is empty if 𝑃 > 2. When 𝑃 = 2, bin(𝜱) consists of sets of the form 𝒗 + ({0} ∪ S) where 𝒗 ∈ V and
      S ⊆ V satisfies #(S) = 2 𝐽 + 1 and B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1 for all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S, 𝒗 1 ≠ 𝒗 2 .
  (b) bin(𝜳 ) consists of sets of the form 𝒗 + L where 𝒗 ∈ V and L ⊆ V satisfies #(L) = 𝑃 𝐽 and B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 0 for
      all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ L.
                                                                       12
Proof. (a) Since ⟨𝝋𝒗1 , 𝝋𝒗2 ⟩ = exp( 2𝑃𝜋i B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 )) is unimodular when 𝒗 1 ≠ 𝒗 2 and ∥𝝋𝒗 ∥ 2 = 𝑃 𝐽 + 1 for all 𝒗, (7) gives
that a subset K of V belongs to bin(𝜱) if and only if both #(K) = 𝑃 𝐽 + 2 and
                   −1 = ⟨𝝋𝒗1 , 𝝋𝒗2 ⟩⟨𝝋𝒗2 , 𝝋𝒗3 ⟩⟨𝝋𝒗3 , 𝝋𝒗1 ⟩ = exp( 2𝑃𝜋i [B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) + B(𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 ) + B(𝒗 3 , 𝒗 1 )])
                                                                 = exp( 2𝑃𝜋i B(𝒗 1 − 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 2 − 𝒗 3 ))
for all pairwise distinct 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 ∈ K. Since −1 is only a 𝑃th root of unity when 𝑃 = 2, bin(𝜱) is thus empty
when 𝑃 > 2. When instead 𝑃 = 2, K ∈ bin(𝜱) if and only if #(K) = 2 𝐽 + 2 and B(𝒗 1 − 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 2 − 𝒗 3 ) = 1 for
all pairwise distinct 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 ∈ K. These conditions are translation invariant, and so K ∈ bin(𝜱) if and only if
K0 := −𝒗 + K ∈ bin(𝜱) for any 𝒗 ∈ V. In particular, for any K ∈ bin(𝜱), letting 𝒗 ∈ K and 𝒗 3 = 0 ∈ K0 , we have
1 = B(𝒗 1 − 0, 𝒗 2 − 0) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) for any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ K0 with 0 ≠ 𝒗 1 ≠ 𝒗 2 ≠ 0. Letting S = K0 \{0}, any K ∈ bin(𝜱)
can thus be written as K = 𝒗 + ({0} ∪ S) where 𝒗 ∈ V, #(S) = 2 𝐽 + 1 and B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1 for all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S with
𝒗 1 ≠ 𝒗 2 . Conversely, for any such S, we claim that K0 := {0} ∪ S belongs to bin(𝜱). Indeed, #(K0 ) = 2 𝐽 + 2 since
#(S) = 2 𝐽 + 1 and 0 ∉ S. Moreover, since 𝑃 = 2, B(𝒗 1 − 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 2 − 𝒗 3 ) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) + B(𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 ) + B(𝒗 3 , 𝒗 1 ) = 1 for all
pairwise distinct 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 ∈ K0 , being either 1 + 1 + 1 = 1 when such 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 are all nonzero, and being a sum of
one 1 and two zeros when any one of these three vectors is 0. Having the claim, we further note that since bin(𝜱) is
translation invariant, it contains 𝒗 + ({0} ∪ S) for any 𝒗 ∈ V and any such S.
    (b) Since ⟨𝝍 𝒗1 , 𝝍 𝒗2 ⟩ = −⟨𝝋𝒗1 , 𝝋𝒗2 ⟩ is unimodular when 𝒗 1 ≠ 𝒗 2 and has ∥𝝍 𝒗 ∥ 2 = 𝑃 𝐽 − 1 for all 𝒗, (7) gives that a
subset K of V belongs to bin(𝜳 ) if and only if both #(K) = 𝑃 𝐽 and
                         −1 = ⟨𝝍 𝒗1 , 𝝍 𝒗2 ⟩⟨𝝍 𝒗2 , 𝝍 𝒗3 ⟩⟨𝝍 𝒗3 , 𝝍 𝒗1 ⟩ = −⟨𝝋𝒗1 , 𝝋𝒗2 ⟩⟨𝝋𝒗2 , 𝝋𝒗3 ⟩⟨𝝋𝒗3 , 𝝋𝒗1 ⟩
                                                                             = − exp( 2𝑃𝜋i B(𝒗 1 − 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 2 − 𝒗 3 )),

for all pairwise distinct 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 ∈ K. As such, K ∈ bin(𝜳 ) if and only if #(K) = 𝑃 𝐽 and 0 = B(𝒗 1 − 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 2 − 𝒗 3 )
for any pairwise distinct 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 ∈ K. As such, K ∈ bin(𝜳 ) if and only if L := −𝒗 + K ∈ bin(𝜳 ) for any 𝒗 ∈ V.
Thus, if K ∈ bin(𝜳 ) then taking 𝒗 ∈ K and 𝒗 3 = 0 ∈ L, we have 0 = B(𝒗 1 − 0, 𝒗 2 − 0) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) for any distinct
nonzero 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ L. Since any 𝒗 ∈ V is orthogonal to 0 and to itself, B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 0 for all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ L. Conversely,
any L ⊆ V with these properties belongs to bin(𝜳 ), having #(L) = 𝑃 𝐽 and B(𝒗 1 − 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 2 − 𝒗 3 ) = 0 − 0 − 0 + 0 = 0
for any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 ∈ V. As such, for any such L, we also have 𝒗 + L ∈ bin(𝜳 ) for any 𝒗 ∈ V.                           □
    In light of the prior result and the fact that orthogonality is simpler than nonorthogonality in general, we for the
moment focus exclusively on the binder of the dual of a symplectic ETF. In particular, a subset S of V is totally
orthogonal if B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 0 for all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S, that is, if S ⊆ S ⊥ . When a subset L of V satisfies L = L ⊥ it is known
as a Lagrangian subspace of V. Any such L is necessarily a subspace of V since S ⊥ is a subspace of V for any
subset S of V. As this name suggests, such sets are topics of classical interest. The following result gives the relevant
folklore. For the sake of completeness, these facts are proven in the appendix.
Lemma 4.4 (Folklore). Let B be a symplectic form on a vector space V of dimension 2𝐽 over a field F. Let S and L
be a subset and subspace of V, respectively, Let 𝑄 be a prime power.
  (a) If S is totally orthogonal then so is span(S).
  (b) If L ⊆ L ⊥ then dim(L) ≤ 𝐽. Here, dim(L) = 𝐽 if and only if L is Lagrangian, that is, L = L ⊥ .
  (c) A finite sequence (𝒗 𝑚 ) 𝑚=1
                               𝑀 of vectors in V is an ordered basis for a totally orthogonal subspace of V if and only
                 ⊥
      if 𝒗 𝑚 ∈ V𝑚 \V𝑚 for each 𝑚 ∈ [𝑀] where V𝑚 := span(𝒗 𝑚′ ) 𝑚  𝑚−1 .

  (d) When F = F𝑄 has order 𝑄, the number of Lagrangian subspaces of V is 𝐽𝑗=1 (𝑄 𝑗 + 1).

Theorem 4.5. For any prime 𝑃 and positive integer 𝐽, the binder of the dual 𝜳 of a 𝑃2𝐽 -vector symplectic ETF 𝜱
(Definition 4.1) consists of all affine Lagrangian subspaces of V, that is, all subsets of V of the form 𝒗 + L where

Proof. By Lemma 4.3, bin(𝜳 ) consists of sets of the form 𝒗 + L where 𝒗 ∈ V and L is a 𝑃 𝐽 -vector totally orthogonal
subset of V. For any such L, Lemma 4.4 gives that span(L) is a totally orthogonal subspace of V and so moreover
that dim(span(L)) ≤ 𝐽. Thus, 𝑃 𝐽 = #(L) ≤ #(span(L)) ≤ 𝑃 𝐽 , implying that L = span(L) is a totally orthogonal
subspace of V with #(L) = 𝑃 𝐽 , i.e., dim(L) = 𝐽. Lemma 4.4 gives that any such L is Lagrangian. Conversely, any
Lagrangian subspace L is totally orthogonal and, by Lemma 4.4, has dim(L) = 𝐽 and so #(L) = 𝑃 𝐽 . Thus indeed,

                                                 bin(𝜳 ) = {𝒗 + L : 𝒗 ∈ V, L ⊆ V, L = L ⊥ }.

    All that remains to be shown is that a subset of bin(𝜳 ) forms an affine plane of order 𝑄 := 𝑃 𝐽 . Here, recall that
any symplectic form on a finite-dimensional vector space has a symplectic basis (12), and that this implies that up
to isomorphism, there is exactly one symplectic form on spaces of a given even dimension 2𝐽 over a given field F.
Without loss of generality we can thus choose V to be F2𝑄 , and let B be the composition of the field trace tr : F𝑄 → F 𝑃
(or any other such nontrivial linear functional) with the canonical F𝑄 -valued symplectic form B1 on this space (13):

                              B = tr ◦ B1 : F𝑄 × F𝑄 → F,                        B((𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ), (𝑦 1 , 𝑦 2 )) = tr(𝑥 1 𝑦 2 − 𝑥2 𝑦 1 ).

Note that the F𝑄 -multiples of any (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ) ≠ (0, 0) form a Lagrangian subspace of F2𝑄 with respect to B: clearly,
{𝑐(𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ) : 𝑐 ∈ F𝑄 } has cardinality 𝑄 = 𝑃 𝐽 , and this set is totally orthogonal since

             B(𝑐 1 (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ), 𝑐 2 (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 )) = B((𝑐 1 𝑥1 , 𝑐 1 𝑥2 ), (𝑐 2 𝑥1 , 𝑐 2 𝑥2 )) = tr(𝑐 1 𝑥1 𝑐 2 𝑥2 − 𝑐 1 𝑥2 𝑐 2 𝑥1 ) = tr(0) = 0

for any 𝑐 1 , 𝑐 2 ∈ F𝑄 . Since bin(𝜳 ) is closed under translation, it thus contains all affine lines (one-dimensional
subspaces) in F2𝑄 , regarded as a plane (two-dimensional space) over F𝑄 , namely the classical (Desarguesian) affine
plane of order 𝑄.                                                                                                   □
Example 4.6. From Example 4.2, recall that when 𝑃 = 2 and 𝐽 = 2, the canonical symplectic form (13) on F42 is
B2 ((𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑥3 , 𝑥4 ), (𝑦 1 , 𝑦 2 , 𝑦 3 , 𝑦 4 )) = (𝑥1 𝑦 2 +𝑥2 𝑦 1 ) + (𝑥 3 𝑦 4 +𝑥 4 𝑦 3 ), and that the dual𝜳 of the corresponding symplectic
ETF 𝜱 has Gram matrix 4I − 𝜞 2 where 𝜞 2 is given by (14). By Theorem 4.5, bin(𝜳 ) consists of all affine Lagrangian
subspaces of F42 , namely all sets of the form 𝒗 + L where L ⊆ F42 satisfies L ⊥ = L, and moreover, that such sets form
a BIBD with parameters (𝑉, 𝐾, Λ, 𝑅, 𝐵) = (22(2) , 22 , 2 + 1, (22 + 1) (2 + 1), 22 (22 + 1) (2 + 1)) = (16, 4, 3, 15, 60). In
particular, there are exactly 15 Lagrangian subspaces of F42 , namely:
                       {0000, 0010, 1000, 1010}, {0000, 0011, 1100, 1111}, {0000, 0111, 1001, 1110}, {0000, 0110, 1011, 1101}, {0000, 0001, 0100, 0101},
                       {0000, 0001, 1000, 1001}, {0000, 0001, 1100, 1101}, {0000, 0010, 0100, 0110}, {0000, 0010, 1100, 1110}, {0000, 0011, 0100, 0111},
                       {0000, 0011, 1000, 1011}, {0000, 0101, 1010, 1111}, {0000, 0101, 1011, 1110}, {0000, 0110, 1001, 1111}, {0000, 0111, 1010, 1101}.
                                                                                                                                                           (16)

These can be obtained, for example, via the iterative construction used in Lemma 4.4. To explain in detail, there are
(16 − 1) (8 − 2) = 90 ordered bases (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) for some Lagrangian subspace of F42 , each consisting of an arbitrary 𝒗 1 ≠ 0
followed by some 𝒗 2 ∈ {𝒗 1 }⊥ \{0, 𝒗 1 }. Any two such ordered bases span the same Lagrangian subspace if and only if
they are equivalent via one of (4 − 1) (4 − 2) = 6 invertible matrices in F22 . Since each of these 90   6 = 15 Lagrangian
subspaces is a subgroup   of F 4 of index 4, it has 4 distinct cosets, yielding 4(15) = 60 affine Lagrangian subspaces

                                                                                     14
    Since bin(𝜳 ) forms a BIBD, we can phase (sign) the entries of the corresponding 60 × 16 incidence matrix à la (10)
to embed its dual 𝜱 into a 10-dimensional subspace of R60 . In fact, Theorem 4.5 guarantees that a subset of bin(𝜳 )
forms an affine plane, namely a BIBD with (𝑉, 𝐾, Λ, 𝑅, 𝐵) = (16, 4, 1, 5, 20), and phasing its 20 × 16 incidence matrix
yields an equally relatively sparse—yet more concise—embedding of 𝜱 into a 10-dimensional subspace of R20 . To
see how such an affine plane arises, let F4 = {0, 1, 𝛼, 𝛽} where 𝛽 = 𝛼2 = 1 + 𝛼, and regard F24 as a two-dimensional
space over F4 that is equipped with the canonical symplectic form (13). Since F24 is two-dimensional, each of its
one-dimensional subspaces is Lagrangian. These spaces are:

            {00, 10, 𝛼0, 𝛽0},        {00, 11, 𝛼𝛼, 𝛽𝛽},                 {00, 1𝛼, 𝛼𝛽, 𝛽1},            {00, 1𝛽, 𝛼1, 𝛽𝛼},             {00, 01, 0𝛼, 0𝛽}.   (17)

Now consider the mapping (𝑥 1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑥3 , 𝑥4 ) ∈ F42 ↦→ (𝑥 1 + 𝑥3 𝛼, 𝑥2 + 𝑥 4 𝛼) ∈ F24 . Under it, the canonical symplectic
form on F42 arises as the composition of that on F24 and the nontrivial linear functional 𝑳 : F4 → F2 defined by
𝑳(0) = 𝑳(𝛼) = 0 and 𝑳(1) = 𝑳(𝛼2 ) = 1:
                                                                                             𝑳
                     (𝑥1 + 𝑥3 𝛼) (𝑦 2 + 𝑦 4 𝛼) + (𝑥2 + 𝑥4 𝛼) (𝑦 1 + 𝑦 3 𝛼) ↦−→ (𝑥1 𝑦 2 + 𝑥2 𝑦 1 ) + (𝑥 3 𝑦 4 + 𝑥4 𝑦 3 ).

In particular, it maps Lagrangian subspaces of F24 over F4 to Lagrangian subspaces of F42 over F2 . Applying it to the
five sets in (17) yields those given in the first row of (16). Since all cosets of the former form a BIBD with Λ = 1, the
same is true of the latter.
    Since the cosets of the 15 Lagrangian subspaces in F42 form a BIBD with (𝑉, 𝐾, Λ, 𝑅, 𝐵) parameters (16, 4, 3, 15, 60),
and the cosets of these particular 5 subspaces form one with parameters (16, 4, 1, 5, 20), the cosets of the remain-
ing 10 Lagrangian subspaces—those given in the second and third rows of (16)—form a BIBD with parameters
(16, 4, 2, 10, 40). We claim that the latter does not resolve into two affine planes. Indeed, if it did, the nonzero members
of these 10 subspaces must resolve into two partitions of F42 \{0000}. One of these must contain {0001, 1000, 1001},
and so the other must contain {0011, 1000, 1011} and {0110, 1001, 1111}, which in turn implies that the intersecting
sets {0101, 1011, 1110} and {0101, 1010, 1111} belong to the same partition, a contradiction.

(much) larger than [5] predicts whenever 𝐽 ≥ 3.
                                                                                15
     Notably, Theorem 5.9 of [5] further shows that when 𝑃 is odd and 𝐽 = 2, bin(𝜳 ) can not be resolved into 𝑃 + 1
affine planes. Moreover, the paragraph of [5] that precedes Corollary 5.11 seemingly doubts whether bin(𝜱) contains
even a single affine plane, again when 𝑃 is odd and 𝐽 = 2. Theorem 4.5 shows that this is unfounded: regardless of 𝑃
and 𝐽, the binder of the dual of any symplectic ETF always contains an affine plane. That said, Example 4.6 suggests
that bin(𝜳 ) does not resolve completely into affine planes even when 𝑃 = 2 and 𝐽 = 2. This begs the question, For
any finite vector space that is equipped with a symplectic form, when can the set of all affine Lagrangian subspaces
of it, less one affine plane, be resolved into proper subsets, each of which forms a BIBD? Being a natural question of
finite symplectic geometry, the answer to it might be found in the classical literature. We leave it for future work.
     Having fully characterized the binder of the dual 𝜳 of any symplectic ETF 𝜱, we devote the remainder of this
section to the binder of 𝜱 itself. By Lemma 4.3, we already know that bin(𝜱) is empty unless 𝑃 = 2. As we now
explain, bin(𝜱) is also empty when 𝑃 = 2 except in two cases, namely when either 𝐽 = 1 or 𝐽 = 2. When 𝑃 = 2 and
𝐽 = 1, this is not surprising: here, 𝜱 is a 4-vector ETF for a space of dimension 3, and so as explained in Example 3.6,
its binder consists of a single copy of V. When instead 𝑃 = 2 and 𝐽 = 2, bin(𝜱) has a remarkable structure.
Theorem 4.8. Let B be a symplectic form on a vector space V over F2 of dimension 2𝐽, and let S ⊆ V satisfy
B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1 for all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S with 𝒗 1 ≠ 𝒗 2 . Then #(S) ≤ 2𝐽 + 1. When 𝑃 = 2, the binder of the corresponding
symplectic ETF 𝜱 (Definition 4.1) is thus empty unless 𝐽 ∈ {1, 2}.
    When 𝑃 = 2 and 𝐽 = 2, bin(𝜱) is nontrivial and so forms a BIBD. It consists of all shifts of any subset V of the
following form, where (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ) is any symplectic basis (12) for V:
                            { 0,   𝒗1,    𝒗2,     𝒗1 + 𝒗2 + 𝒗3,      𝒗1 + 𝒗2 + 𝒗4,      𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 3 + 𝒗 4 }.                   (18)
Proof. We prove the first claim by induction on 𝐽.            When 𝐽 = 1, V contains 22𝐽 = 4 vectors, and so S necessarily
satisfies #(S) ≤ 2𝐽 + 1 = 3 or else S = V contains both 0 and 𝒗 ≠ 0 where B(0, 𝒗) = 0. Now suppose this claim
holds for a given positive integer 𝐽 − 1, that B is a symplectic form on a vector space V over F2 of dimension 2𝐽 and
that S ⊆ V satisfies B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1 for all distinct 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S. We show that #(S) ≤ 2𝐽 + 1. This obviously holds if
#(S) ≤ 2. When instead #(S) > 2, take any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S, 𝒗 1 ≠ 𝒗 2 . Since B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1, (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) is a symplectic pair,
and so V is an internal direct sum of V0 := {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }⊥ and V0⊥ = span{𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }. The set S0 := 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + S\{𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 } has
#(S0 ) = #(S) − 2. Moreover, S0 ⊆ V0 since
                 B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗) = 0 + 1 + 1 = 0 = 1 + 0 + 1 = B(𝒗 2 , 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗),         ∀ 𝒗 ∈ S\{𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }.
In particular, B(𝒗 3 , 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) = B(𝒗 4 , 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) = 0 for any 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ∈ S0 . At the same time, when such 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 are distinct
then 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 3 and 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 4 are distinct members of S, implying
                           1 = B(𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 4 ) = 0 + 0 + 0 + B(𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ) = B(𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ).
Since dim(V0 ) = dim(V) −2 = 2𝐽 −2 = 2(𝐽 −1), applying the inductive hypothesis to S0 gives #(S0 ) ≤ 2(𝐽 −1) +1 =
2𝐽 − 1, and so #(S) = #(S0 ) + 2 ≤ 2𝐽 + 1.
      Now recall from Lemma 4.3 that bin(𝜱) consists of sets of the form 𝒗 + ({0} ∪ S) where #(S) = 2 𝐽 + 1 and
B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1 for all distinct 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S. From the first claim, such a set S can only exist if 2 𝐽 + 1 ≤ 2𝐽 + 1, namely
𝐽 ∈ {1, 2}. As such, bin(𝜱) is empty when 𝐽 > 2.
      When 𝐽 = 2, bin(𝜱) consists of all sets of the form 𝒗 + ({0} ∪ S) where #(S) = 5 and B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1 for all distinct
𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S. For any distinct 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S, the inductive argument above gives that S0 := 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + S\{𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 } consists of
the three nonzero members of V0 := {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }⊥ . That is, S0 = {𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 , 𝒗 3 + 𝒗 4 } where (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ) is a symplectic
basis for V, implying that indeed {0} ∪ S is of form (18). In particular, 𝜱 is a DTETF whose binder is nonempty,
implying by Theorem 3.5 that it forms a BIBD.                                                                                         □
    To be clear, when 𝑃 = 2 and 𝐽 = 2, one may of course use a rigorous computer algebra system to explicitly compute
        of the symplectic ETF 𝜱. For example, recalling the matrix 𝜞 2 of (14), one may simply compute which of
the binder
the 16
     6 = 8008 principal submatrices of 4I + 𝜞 2 of size 6 are singular. Doing so reveals just 16 sets, all translates of
a single 6-element subset of V. Together, these sets form a BIBD with parameters (𝑉, 𝐾, Λ, 𝑅, 𝐵) = (16, 6, 2, 6, 16)
with a V-circulant incidence matrix, and so arise from a difference set for V. Notably, this requires that any set of the
form (18) for one choice of symplectic basis is simply a shift of the analogous set for any other choice of such a basis.
In the next section, we give an elementary proof of this fact that is an offshoot of our investigation into the binders of
certain sub-ETFs of a symplectic ETF and its dual in the special case where 𝑃 = 2.
                                                                    16
5. The binders of equiangular tight frames arising from quadratic forms
    In this section, we calculate the binder of any member of four particular infinite families of real DTETFs that arise
from quadratic forms over the binary field F2 := {0, 1}. ETFs in one of these four families consist of 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 − 1)
vectors that span a space of dimension 31 (2 𝐽 −1 − 1) (2 𝐽 − 1), where 𝐽 is some positive integer, e.g., 28 vectors in R7 .
ETFs in another consist of 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1) vectors that span a space of dimension 31 (2 𝐽 −1 + 1) (2 𝐽 + 1), e.g., 36 vectors in
R15 . The remaining two families consist of their duals, which notably both span spaces of dimension 13 (22𝐽 − 1), e.g.,
28 and 36 vectors in R21 . The existence of real ETFs with such parameters has been known for some time, including
for example Steiner [25] and Tremain [17] ETFs arising from a Steiner triple system on 2 𝐽 − 1 vertices, respectively.
Recently, it was shown that ETFs with these parameters arise as sub-ETFs (or duals of sub-ETFs) of symplectic ETFs
and their duals over F2 [15]. This leads to an infinite family [11] of real mutually unbiased ETFs [22]. Moreover,
real DTETFs with these parameters exist [28, 12], arising from actions of certain symplectic groups on certain sets of
quadratic forms. By exploiting some known but obscure connections [29, 38] between such actions and the ETFs that
were constructed in [15], we verify that the latter are indeed DTETFs. We then exploit their connections to concepts
from Section 4 in conjunction with Theorem 3.5 to calculate their binders. Specifically, we exploit Theorem 4.5 to
show that ETFs in two of these families have nonempty binders whose corresponding BIBDs relate to each other—and
to the corresponding BIBD of affine Lagrangian subspaces—in interesting ways, and use Theorem 4.8 to show that
the binders of ETFs in the remaining two families are empty except in a finite number of cases. We now review the
parts of the theory of quadratic forms over F2 that we shall need.

5.1. Quadratic forms over F2
     Let V be a vector space over a field F. The definition of a quadratic form on V depends on whether char(F) = 2, i.e.,
if 1 + 1 = 0 ∈ F. When char(F) ≠ 2, quadratic forms equate to symmetric bilinear forms. There, a function Q : V → F
is a quadratic form if there exists a bilinear form B on V for which Q(𝑥1 𝒗 1 +𝑥 2 𝒗 2 ) = 𝑥12 Q(𝒗 1 ) +2𝑥 1 𝑥2 B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) +𝑥22 Q(𝒗 2 )
for all 𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ∈ F and 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V. This B is unique and symmetric, having B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 12 [Q(𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) − Q(𝒗 1 ) − Q(𝒗 2 )]
for any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V. Conversely, any symmetric bilinear form B on V arises from the quadratic form Q(𝒗) := B(𝒗, 𝒗).
This Q is unique since necessarily Q(𝒗) = Q( 21 𝒗 + 21 𝒗) = 41 Q(𝒗) + 12 B(𝒗, 𝒗) + 41 Q(𝒗) for all 𝒗 ∈ V. When instead
char(F) = 2, it is common practice to modify this definition in a seemingly minor way: a function Q : V → F is a
quadratic form if there exists a bilinear form B on V for which

                                         Q(𝑥 1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥 2 𝒗 2 ) = 𝑥12 Q(𝒗 1 ) + 𝑥1 𝑥2 B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) + 𝑥22 Q(𝒗 2 )                                    (19)

for all 𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ∈ F, 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V. As before, Q uniquely determines B: letting 𝑥1 = 𝑥2 = 1 here gives

                                       B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = Q(𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) + Q(𝒗 1 ) + Q(𝒗 2 ),        ∀ 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V.                                   (20)

Any such B is necessarily alternating and Q(0) = 0: note B(𝒗, 𝒗) = Q(𝒗 + 𝒗) + Q(𝒗) + Q(𝒗) = Q(0) for all 𝒗 ∈ V,
and so B(𝒗, 𝒗) = Q(0) = B(0, 0) = 0 for all 𝒗 ∈ V. Since −1 = 1, such an alternating form B is also symmetric.
Remarkably, in this setting, the quadratic form Q that gives rise to a bilinear form B is not necessarily unique. For
example, since (𝑥 + 𝑦) 2 = 𝑥 2 + 𝑦 2 for any 𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ F, the canonical symplectic form ((𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ), (𝑦 1 , 𝑦 2 )) ↦→ 𝑥1 𝑦 2 + 𝑥2 𝑦 1
on F2 arises from two distinct quadratic forms, namely (𝑥 1 , 𝑥2 ) ↦→ 𝑥1 𝑥 2 and (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ) ↦→ 𝑥12 + 𝑥1 𝑥 2 + 𝑥22 :

   [(𝑥1 + 𝑦 1 ) 2 + (𝑥 1 + 𝑦 1 ) (𝑥2 + 𝑦 2 ) + (𝑥 2 + 𝑦 2 ) 2 ] + (𝑥12 + 𝑥1 𝑥2 + 𝑥22 ) + (𝑦 21 + 𝑦 1 𝑦 2 + 𝑦 22 )
                                                                                = (𝑥 1 + 𝑦 1 ) (𝑥 2 + 𝑦 2 ) + 𝑥1 𝑥2 + 𝑦 1 𝑦 2 = 𝑥1 𝑦 2 + 𝑥2 𝑦 1 .   (21)

    This theory simplifies when F is F2 . A function Q : V → F2 is a quadratic form if and only if (20) defines a
bilinear form B, as this B satisfies (19) for any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V when 𝑥 1 = 𝑥2 = 1, and any bilinear form satisfies (19) for
any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V when either 𝑥1 = 0 or 𝑥 2 = 0. When this occurs, we say that Q yields B. If Q1 , Q2 : V → F give rise
to the same bilinear form B then Q1 + Q2 is linear: Q1 + Q2 distributes over multiplication by scalars in F2 = {0, 1}
since (Q1 + Q2 )(0) = 0 + 0 = 0, and distributes over addition:

                      (Q1 + Q2 ) (𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) + Q1 (𝒗 1 ) + Q1 (𝒗 2 ) + B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) + Q2 (𝒗 1 ) + Q2 (𝒗 2 )
                                                 = (Q1 + Q2 ) (𝒗 1 ) + (Q1 + Q2 ) (𝒗 2 ),         ∀ 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V.

                                                                           17
Conversely, if Q : V → F2 is a quadratic form that yields B and 𝑳 : V → F2 is linear, then Q𝑳 := Q + 𝑳 is a quadratic
form that also yields B since

              Q𝑳 (𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) + Q𝑳 (𝒗 1 ) + Q𝑳 (𝒗 2 ) = Q(𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) + Q(𝒗 1 ) + Q(𝒗 2 ) + 𝑳(2𝒗 1 + 2𝒗 2 ) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 )

for any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V. Thus, when Q yields B, the set of all quadratic forms that yield B is the affine linear subspace
Q + {𝑳 : V → F2 | 𝑳 is linear} of F2V , a set whose cardinality equals that of V.
    For the remainder of this section, let V be a vector space over F2 of dimension 2𝐽, and let Q be a quadratic form
on V whose bilinear form (20) is nondegenerate and so symplectic. Forms of this type arise in the constructions of
the maximum number of real mutually unbiased bases in spaces whose dimension is a power of 4 [9, 7]. Here, any
linear functional 𝑳 : V → F2 has a Riesz representation— there exists a unique 𝒗 𝑳 ∈ V such that 𝑳(𝒗) = B(𝒗 𝑳 , 𝒗)
for all 𝒗 ∈ V—implying

                        Q𝑳 (𝒗) = (Q + 𝑳) (𝒗) = Q(𝒗) + B(𝒗 𝑳 , 𝒗) = Q(𝒗 + 𝒗 𝑳 ) + Q(𝒗 𝑳 ),                  ∀ 𝒗 ∈ V.                  (22)

Saying that a given 𝒗 ∈ V is singular (with respect to Q) when Q(𝒗) = 0, we thus see that the quadratic forms that
yield B partition into two types: shifts of Q by singular 𝒗, and shifts of Q + 1 by nonsingular 𝒗. The number of the
former is thus the size of the quadric Q of Q, defined as

                                                      Q := {𝒗 ∈ V : Q(𝒗) = 0}.                                                       (23)

Since Q is F2 -valued, it can be recovered from Q. As noted above, Q necessarily contains 0. We moreover claim
that Q ≠ {0} whenever 𝐽 ≥ 2. Indeed, for any 𝒗 1 ∈ V\{0}, {𝒗 1 }⊥ has dimension 2𝐽 − 1 ≥ 3, and so there exists
𝒗 2 ∈ {𝒗 1 }⊥ \{0, 𝒗 1 }. Since (20) gives 0 = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = Q(𝒗 1 ) + Q(𝒗 2 ) + Q(𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ), either exactly one or exactly three
of the three distinct vectors 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 and 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 are singular, giving the claim.
     Now recall that if (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) is any symplectic pair in V then V is the internal direct sum of V0 := {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }⊥ and
V0⊥ = span{𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }, and also that the restriction B0 of B to V0 is a symplectic form. The restriction Q0 : V0 → F2 of
Q : V → F2 to V0 is thus a quadratic form that yields B0 . Here, writing any 𝒗 ∈ V as 𝒗 = 𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 where
𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ∈ F2 and 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 , (19) gives

                          Q(𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 ) = Q(𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 ) + B(𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 0 ) + Q(𝒗 0 )
                                                    = 𝑥12 Q(𝒗 1 ) + 𝑥1 𝑥2 B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) + 𝑥22 Q(𝒗 2 ) + 0 + Q0 (𝒗 0 )
                                                    = 𝑥12 Q(𝒗 1 ) + 𝑥1 𝑥2 + 𝑥22 Q(𝒗 2 ) + Q0 (𝒗 0 ).                                 (24)

     A pair (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) of vectors in V is hyperbolic if B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1 and Q(𝒗 1 ) = 0 = Q(𝒗 2 ), that is, if it is a singular
symplectic pair. We claim that any nonzero 𝒗 1 ∈ Q belongs to such a pair. Indeed, for any 𝒗 ∈ V\{𝒗 1 }⊥ , we have
B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 1 + 𝒗) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗) = 1 where (20) gives 1 = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗) = 0 + Q(𝒗) + Q(𝒗 1 + 𝒗), implying one member of {𝒗, 𝒗 1 + 𝒗}
is singular while the other is not. Letting 𝒗 2 be the former, the latter is 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 . Having the claim, we next recall that
if 𝐽 ≥ 2 then Q ≠ {0} and so it contains such a pair. When instead 𝐽 = 1, V either contains such a pair or it does not,
and so either Q = {0, 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 } or Q = {0}, respectively. In the former case, (19) gives Q(𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥 2 𝒗 2 ) = 𝑥 1 𝑥2 for any
𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ∈ F2 . In the latter case, (19) instead gives Q(𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥 2 𝒗 2 ) = 𝑥12 + 𝑥1 𝑥2 + 𝑥22 for any 𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ∈ F2 , where 𝒗 1 and 𝒗 2
are any distinct nonzero vectors in V. In either case, when 𝐽 = 1, (21) implies that the resulting bilinear form is the
sole symplectic form on V, having B(𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 , 𝑦 1 𝒗 1 + 𝑦 2 𝒗 2 ) = 𝑥1 𝑦 2 + 𝑥2 𝑦 1 for any 𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑦 1 , 𝑦 2 ∈ F2 , meaning any
distinct nonzero vectors in V are nonorthogonal.
     If (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) is a hyperbolic pair in V then (24) simplifies to

                                               Q(𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 ) = 𝑥 1 𝑥2 + Q0 (𝒗 0 )                                        (25)

for any 𝑥 1 , 𝑥2 ∈ F2 and 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 . In particular, 𝒗 ∈ Q if and only if either 𝑥1 𝑥2 = 0 = Q0 (𝒗 0 ) or 𝑥1 𝑥2 = 1 = Q(𝒗 0 ), that
is, if and only if either (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ) ∈ {(0, 0), (1, 0), (0, 1)} and 𝒗 0 ∈ Q0 := {𝒗 0′ ∈ V0 : Q0 (𝒗 0′ ) = 0} or (𝑥 1 , 𝑥2 ) = (1, 1)
and 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 \Q0 . Thus, Q partitions as

                                    Q = Q0 ⊔ (𝒗 1 + Q0 ) ⊔ (𝒗 2 + Q0 ) ⊔ (𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + V0 \Q0 ).                                      (26)
                                                                     18
The sizes of Q and Q0 are thus related by #(Q) = 3#(Q0 ) + [#(V0 ) − #(Q0 )] = 22𝐽 −2 + 2#(Q0 ). As such,
#(Q) = 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 ± 1) if and only if #(Q0 ) is the analogous value when “𝐽” is 𝐽 − 1, i.e.,

                         #(Q0 ) = 12 #(Q) − 22𝐽 −3 = 2 𝐽 −2 (2 𝐽 ± 1) − 22𝐽 −3 = 2 ( 𝐽 −1) −1 (2 𝐽 −1 ± 1).

Moreover, such an equation holds when 𝐽 = 1: here, recall Q is either {0} or of the form {0, 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 } for some hyperbolic
pair (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ), and so #(Q) is either 1 = 21−1 (21 − 1) or 3 = 21−1 (21 + 1), respectively. As such, any quadratic form
Q : V → F2 has a sign, denoted sign(Q), such that

                    sign(Q) := 2− ( 𝐽 −1) [#(Q) − 22𝐽 −1 ] ∈ {1, −1},          #(Q) = 2 𝐽 −1 [2 𝐽 + sign(Q)],              (27)

and it is either 1 or −1 when, after iteratively choosing 𝐽 − 1 hyperbolic pairs from V, each lying in the orthogonal
complement of those previously chosen, the restriction of Q to the remaining two-dimensional space has either exactly
three singular points or exactly one such point, respectively. Iteratively choosing hyperbolic pairs in this way for
                                                             2𝐽
as long as possible yields a symplectic basis (12) (𝒗 𝑚 ) 𝑚=1     for V such that either Q(𝒗 𝑚 ) = 0 for all 𝑚 ∈ [2𝐽]—a
hyperbolic basis—or Q(𝒗 𝑚 ) = 0 for all 𝑚 ∈ [2𝐽 − 2] but Q(𝒗 2𝐽 −1 ) = Q(𝒗 2𝐽 ) = 1, depending on whether Q is positive
or negative, respectively, that is, whether sign(Q) is 1 or −1, respectively. Here, iteratively applying (25) gives Q(𝑽𝒙)

respectively. Both of these forms yield the canonical symplectic form (13) on F2𝐽     2 . Thus, up to isomorphism, there
is a unique symplectic form on V, and a unique quadratic form of positive sign, as well as a unique one of negative
sign, that give rise to it. Now recall that there are exactly #(V) = 22𝐽 distinct quadratic forms on V that yield B, each
corresponding to a particular choice of 𝒗 𝑳 in (22). The quadric of such a form Q𝑳 is related to that of Q as follows:
                                                                              

                       Q 𝑳 := {𝒗 ∈ V : Q𝑳 (𝒗) = Q(𝒗 + 𝒗 𝑳 ) + Q(𝒗 𝑳 ) = 0} =                                         (29)
                                                                                𝒗 𝑳 + Qc, 𝒗 𝑳 ∈ Qc .

In particular, shifting Q by a member of itself yields the quadric of a quadratic form with the same sign as that of Q,
while shifting it by a member of Q c yields the complement of a quadric of a quadratic form whose sign is the opposite
of that of Q. There are thus exactly 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1) positive quadratic forms on V that yield B, and exactly 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 − 1)
negative such forms.

5.2. Doubly transitive equiangular tight frames that arise from quadratic forms over F2
    We now explain the construction of [15], and then use ideas from [29, 38] to show that the resulting ETFs are
DTETFs. By Theorem 1.3 of [28], DTETFs with these parameters are known to exist and are unique up to equivalence,
so the contribution of this subsection is the fact that they can be constructed in the manner of [15]. In the next subsection,
we use this fact to calculate their binders and moreover relate these binders to those of the corresponding symplectic
ETF and its dual. It turns out that a key concept of this work is how the quadrics of various quadratic forms are related
to each other, and to the complements of each other:
Definition 5.1. Let B be a symplectic form on a vector space V over F2 of dimension 2𝐽. We say that a subset D of
V is an affine quadric if either it or its complement is the quadric (23) of some quadratic form Q that yields B.

     Later on, we justify this terminology by verifying that any affine quadric is a shift of a quadric. By (27), the size of
any affine quadric D is either 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1) or 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 − 1). In either case, D c is an affine quadric of the other size.
We use this concept to streamline Theorems 4.4 and 4.5 of [15]: if D is any affine quadric, then the D × V, D c × V,
D × D c and D c × D submatrices of the character table 𝜞 are synthesis maps of ETFs, and moreover the duals of the
latter two are yet other ETFs. A proof of this reimagining of ideas from [15] is given in the appendix.

                                                                19
Lemma 5.2 ([15]). Let B be a symplectic form on a vector space V over F2 of dimension 2𝐽 ≥ 4, and let 𝜞 ∈ R V ×V ,
𝜞(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) := (−1) B(𝒗1 ,𝒗2 ) be its corresponding character table. Three distinct pairs of dual ETFs arise from any affine
quadric D in V (Definition 5.1) with #(D) = 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1):
  (a) 22𝐽 -vector ETFs for spaces of dimension 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1) and 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 − 1): the finite sequences 𝜱 = (𝝋𝒗 )𝒗 ∈ V in
