    In general, an ETF is harmonic when its synthesis map arises by extracting rows from the character table of a finite
abelian group G. This occurs if and only if the indices of these rows form a difference set D for G [36, 32, 34, 39, 13],
namely when there exists Λ ≥ 0 such that #{(𝑑1 , 𝑑2 ) ∈ D × D : 𝑔 = 𝑑1 − 𝑑2 } = Λ for all 𝑔 ∈ G\{0}. This is the case
for the ETFs of Lemma 5.2(a), for example [15]. Many harmonic ETFs have irrational Welch bounds and thus empty
binders [16]. That said, every harmonic ETF has a group-circulant Gram matrix. For example, as seen in the proof
of Lemma 5.2, the finite sequence (𝝋𝒗 )𝒗 ∈ V of (a) has ⟨𝝋𝒗1 , 𝝋𝒗2 ⟩ = (−1) Q(𝒗1 +𝒗2 ) whenever 𝒗 1 ≠ 𝒗 2 , where Q is some
quadratic form, and so ⟨𝝋𝒗+𝒗1 , 𝝋𝒗+𝒗2 ⟩ = ⟨𝝋𝒗1 , 𝝋𝒗2 ⟩ for all 𝒗, 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V. The symmetry group of a harmonic ETF
thus contains all translations. In particular, the binder of any harmonic ETF is translation-invariant: when a harmonic
ETF contains a regular simplex, any shift of its indices yields another. The binders of some harmonic ETFs seemingly
consist of all cosets of a single subgroup of the underlying group [16, 26]. Such incidence structures are not BIBDs. It is
therefore remarkable that the binder of the harmonic ETF (𝝍 𝒗 )𝒗 ∈ V of Lemma 5.2(a) does form a BIBD: it is equivalent
to the dual of the corresponding symplectic ETF, and so the two share the same binder which, by Theorem 4.5, forms a
BIBD and consists of all affine Lagrangian subspaces of V. (As expected, this binder is translation-invariant.) Apart
from these ETFs, the 𝐽 = 2 instance of (𝝋𝒗 )𝒗 ∈ V of Lemma 5.2(a)—see Theorem 4.8—and trivial harmonic ETFs that
arise from singleton difference sets of their complements, we know of no other harmonic ETFs whose binders happen
to form BIBDs. This raises the question as to whether such objects exist. We leave this problem for future work.
    Since any symplectic ETF and its dual are DTETFs [27], the equivalent ETFs of Lemma 5.2(a) are too. We show
that the ETFs of Lemma 5.2(b) and (c) are DTETFs as well. The key idea is a known but obscure [29, 38] way in
which the symplectic group (15) here acts, in a doubly-transitive but not straightforward way, on the members of any
affine quadric. To understand that action, it helps to better understand affine quadrics themselves:
Lemma 5.3. Let B be a symplectic form on a vector space V over F2 of dimension 2𝐽. Then the size of any affine
quadric is either 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1) or 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 − 1), and there are exactly 22𝐽 affine quadrics of each such size, being the
necessarily-distinct shifts of the quadric (23) of any particular quadratic form Q that satisfies (20) and whose sign (27)
is positive or negative, respectively.
Proof. By Definition 5.1, if D is an affine quadric then either D = Q or D = Q c where Q is the quadric (23) of
some quadratic form Q that satisfies (20). By (27), we thus have either #(D) = #(Q) = 2 𝐽 −1 [2 𝐽 + sign(Q)] or
#(D) = #(Q c ) = 22𝐽 − #(Q) = 2 𝐽 −1 [2 𝐽 − sign(Q)].

                                                                  20
    We claim that every shift of the quadric Q of any particular such Q is an affine quadric. Indeed, for any 𝒗 𝑳 ∈ V,
either 𝒗 𝑳 ∈ Q or 𝒗 𝑳 ∈ Q c . In the former case, (29) gives that 𝒗 𝑳 + Q = Q 𝑳 is the quadric of the quadratic form Q𝑳
given in (22). In the latter case, (29) instead gives 𝒗 𝑳 + Q = (𝒗 𝑳 + Q c ) c = Q 𝑳c , giving the claim. We moreover claim
that any two such shifts are necessarily distinct. Indeed, if 𝒗 𝑳 + Q = Q then 𝒗 𝑳 = 𝒗 𝑳 + 0 ∈ 𝒗 𝑳 + Q = Q, implying
by (29) that Q 𝑳 = 𝒗 𝑳 + Q = Q. Since any F2 -valued quadratic form is uniquely determined by its quadric—mapping
members of its quadric to 0 and all other vectors to 1—this implies Q𝑳 = Q, implying by (22) that B(𝒗 𝑳 , 𝒗) = 0 for all
𝒗 ∈ V and thus 𝒗 𝑳 = 0, giving the claim.
    Since B arises from quadratic forms of both possible signs, there are thus at least 22𝐽 distinct affine quadrics of
each of the two possible sizes. To see that these are the only affine quadrics in V, now let D be any affine quadric
whose size equals that of Q. Then either D or D c is the quadric Q ′ of a quadratic form Q′ that satisfies (20). Since
every such quadratic form that satisfies (20) is of the form given in (22), we thus have Q′ = Q𝑳 for some 𝒗 𝑳 ∈ V. As
such, D is either Q 𝑳 or Q 𝑳c where (29) gives that Q 𝑳 is either 𝒗 𝑳 + Q or 𝒗 𝑳 + Q c . Thus, D itself is either 𝒗 𝑳 + Q or
𝒗 𝑳 + Q c . Since D and Q have the same size, this implies D = 𝒗 𝑳 + Q is a shift of Q.                                   □
     As we now explain, in this context—where B is a symplectic form on a finite-dimensional vector space V over
the field F2 —the symplectic group Sp(B) of (15) acts in a doubly transitive way on the set of all quadratic forms of a
particular sign that yield B, and moreover this action equates to one by this same group on any affine quadric. To be
precise, for any 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B) and any quadratic from Q that yields B, the mapping 𝑨·Q : V → F2 , ( 𝑨·Q) (𝒗) := Q( 𝑨 −1 𝒗)
is itself a quadratic form that yields B: since any 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B) is an invertible linear operator on V that preserves B,
            ( 𝑨 · Q) (𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) + ( 𝑨 · Q) (𝒗 1 ) + ( 𝑨 · Q) (𝒗 2 ) = Q( 𝑨 −1 𝒗 1 + 𝑨 −1 𝒗 2 ) + Q( 𝑨 −1 𝒗 1 ) + Q( 𝑨 −1 𝒗 2 )
                                                                     = Q( 𝑨 −1 (𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 )) + Q( 𝑨 −1 𝒗 1 ) + Q( 𝑨 −1 𝒗 2 )
                                                                     = B( 𝑨 −1 𝒗 1 , 𝑨 −1 𝒗 2 )
                                                                     = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ),
for any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V. It is now straightforward to show that
                                          ( 𝑨, Q) ↦→ 𝑨 · Q,        ( 𝑨 · Q) (𝒗) := Q( 𝑨 −1 𝒗),                                     (30)
defines an action of Sp(B) on the set of all quadratic forms that yield B. Here, the quadric of 𝑨 · Q is obtained by
applying 𝑨 to the elements of the quadric Q of Q:
                    𝑨Q := { 𝑨𝒗 : 𝒗 ∈ Q} = {𝒗 ′ ∈ V : Q( 𝑨 −1 𝒗 ′ ) = 0} = {𝒗 ′ ∈ V : ( 𝑨 · Q) (𝒗 ′ ) = 0}.
Since 𝑨 is invertible and so #( 𝑨Q) = #(Q), this implies that the sign (27) of 𝑨 · Q equals that of Q. As such, the
aforementioned action of Sp(B) on the set of all quadratic forms that yield B is not transitive, and in fact restricts
to an action on the set of all such forms of either given sign. Remarkably, when restricted in this way, this action
becomes doubly transitive. This fact is briefly mentioned in [8]. See [38] for an overview of it, its relation to earlier
similar results [29], and additional context about quadratic forms over F2 in general. For the sake of completeness, an
elementary, self-contained proof of this fact is given in the appendix:
Lemma 5.4 ([29, 8]). Let B be a symplectic form on a vector space V over F2 of dimension 2𝐽, and let Sp(B) be the
corresponding symplectic group (15). Then (30) is a well-defined doubly transitive action of Sp(B) on the set of all
quadratic forms Q that yield B of either given sign (27).
    We exploit this fact in the manner of [38]. To explain, let Q be the quadric of any quadratic form Q that yields
B. For any 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B), we have that 𝑨Q is the quadric of the quadratic form 𝑨 · Q which also yields B and has
sign( 𝑨 · Q) = sign(Q). By (22) and (29), there thus exists a unique 𝒗 𝑨 ∈ Q such that 𝑨Q = Q + 𝒗 𝑨 , that is, such
that 𝑨Q + 𝒗 𝑨 = Q. Since Q is F2 -valued and 𝒗 ∈ 𝑨Q + 𝒗 𝑨 if and only if Q( 𝑨 −1 (𝒗 + 𝒗 𝑨 )) = 0, this equates to having
Q( 𝑨 −1 (𝒗 + 𝒗 𝑨 )) = Q(𝒗) for all 𝒗 ∈ V, that is, to having Q(𝒗) = Q( 𝑨𝒗 + 𝒗 𝑨 ) for all 𝒗 ∈ V. As such, the mapping
                                                     ( 𝑨, 𝒗) ↦→ 𝑨 · 𝒗 := 𝑨𝒗 + 𝒗 𝑨                                                  (31)
is a bijection from V onto itself that restricts to bijections from Q and Q c onto themselves. We claim that the
mapping (31) is in fact the action (30) under the bijection 𝒗 𝑳 ↦→ Q𝑳 from V onto the set of all quadratic forms
                                                                    21
that yield B that is described in (22). Indeed, under this bijection, any 𝒗 𝑳 ∈ V maps to the quadratic form
𝒗 ↦→ Q𝑳 (𝒗) := Q(𝒗) + B(𝒗 𝑳 , 𝒗) for all 𝒗 ∈ V. Acting on Q𝑳 by 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B) gives

                                 𝒗 ↦→ ( 𝑨 · Q𝑳 ) (𝒗) = Q𝑳 ( 𝑨 −1 𝒗) = Q( 𝑨 −1 𝒗) + B(𝒗 𝑳 , 𝑨 −1 𝒗).

To simplify further, recall that Q( 𝑨 −1 𝒗) = Q(𝒗 + 𝒗 𝑨 ) for all 𝒗 ∈ V, that B( 𝑨𝒗 1 , 𝑨𝒗 2 ) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) for all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V,
that 𝒗 𝑨 ∈ Q, and moreover recall (20):

                             Q( 𝑨 −1 𝒗) + B(𝒗 𝑳 , 𝑨 −1 𝒗) = Q(𝒗 + 𝒗 𝑨 ) + B( 𝑨𝒗 𝑳 , 𝒗)
                                                               = B(𝒗, 𝒗 𝑨 ) + Q(𝒗) + Q(𝒗 𝑨 ) + B( 𝑨𝒗 𝑳 , 𝒗)
                                                               = Q(𝒗) + B( 𝑨𝒗 𝑳 + 𝒗 𝑨 , 𝒗).

Pulling 𝒗 ↦→ ( 𝑨 · Q𝑳 ) (𝒗) = Q(𝒗) + B( 𝑨𝒗 𝑳 + 𝒗 𝑨 , 𝒗) back through the bijection 𝒗 𝑳 ↦→ Q𝑳 gives 𝑨 · 𝒗 𝑳 = 𝑨𝒗 𝑳 + 𝒗 𝑨 ,
namely the result of acting on 𝒗 𝑳 by 𝑨 via (31), as claimed. Since (30) is a group action, the equivalent mapping (31) is
as well. Moreover, further recalling that (27) and (29) give sign(Q𝑳 ) = sign(Q) if and only if 𝒗 𝑳 ∈ Q, the restrictions
of the action (31) to Q and Q c equate to the restrictions of (30) to the sets of quadratic forms on V that yield B
whose signs equal sign(Q) and − sign(Q), respectively. Since Lemma 5.4 gives that the latter are doubly transitive,
the former are as well. We summarize these facts from [38] as follows:
Lemma 5.5 ([38]). Let B be a symplectic form on a vector space V over F2 of dimension 2𝐽, and let Sp(B) be the
corresponding symplectic group (15). Then letting Q be the quadric of any quadratic form Q on V that yields B, for
any 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B) there exists a unique 𝒗 𝑨 ∈ Q such that

                                𝑨Q + 𝒗 𝑨 = Q,              i.e.,      Q(𝒗) = Q( 𝑨𝒗 + 𝒗 𝑨 ),           ∀ 𝒗 ∈ V.                  (32)

Moreover, (31) defines an action of Sp(B) on V whose restrictions to Q and Q c are doubly transitive.

    These facts provide a quick proof of the following result:
Theorem 5.6. The ETFs of Lemma 5.2(b) and (c) are DTETFs.
Proof. By Definition 5.1, there exists a quadratic form Q that yields B whose quadric Q is either D or D c . By
Lemma 5.5, for every 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B) there exists 𝒗 𝑨 ∈ Q such that (32) holds, and moreover (31) defines a doubly
transitive action on both Q and Q c , that is, on both D and D c . Identifying any 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B) with the restriction of the
mapping 𝒗 ↦→ 𝑨 · 𝒗 = 𝑨𝒗 + 𝒗 𝑨 to D and D c thus yields subgroups of Sym(D) and Sym(D c ) that are isomorphic
to Sp(B) and whose natural actions on D and D c , respectively, are doubly transitive. They are the affine symplectic
groups of [38]. Since the ETFs of Lemma 5.2(c) and (b) are indexed by D and D c respectively, it thus suffices to
show that the members of these subgroups of Sym(D) and Sym(D c ) belong to the symmetry groups of these ETFs,
respectively. To show this, note that for any 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B), and any 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ V that both lie in either D or D c ,

                  B( 𝑨 · 𝒗 1 , 𝑨 · 𝒗 2 ) = B( 𝑨𝒗 1 + 𝒗 𝑨 , 𝑨𝒗 2 + 𝒗 𝑨 ) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) + B( 𝑨𝒗 1 , 𝒗 𝑨 ) + B(𝒗 𝑨 , 𝑨𝒗 2 )

and so defining unimodular scalars (𝑧 𝒗 )𝒗 ∈ V by 𝑧 𝒗 := (−1) B(𝒗 𝑨 , 𝑨𝒗) for all 𝒗 ∈ V, we have

                         (−1) B( 𝑨·𝒗1 , 𝑨·𝒗2 ) = (−1) B( 𝑨𝒗1 ,𝒗 𝑨 )+B(𝒗 𝑨 , 𝑨𝒗2 )+B(𝒗1 ,𝒗2 ) = 𝑧 𝒗1 𝑧 𝒗2 (−1) B(𝒗1 ,𝒗2 ) .

When combined with Lemma 5.2(c) and (b), this gives ⟨𝝍 𝑨·𝒗1 , 𝝍 𝑨·𝒗2 ⟩ = 𝑧 𝒗1 𝑧 𝒗2 ⟨𝝍 𝒗1 , 𝝍 𝒗2 ⟩ for all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ D, and
⟨𝝋 𝑨·𝒗1 , 𝝋 𝑨·𝒗2 ⟩ = 𝑧 𝒗1 𝑧 𝒗2 ⟨𝝋𝒗1 , 𝝋𝒗2 ⟩ for all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ D c , respectively.                                        □
    Since the ETFs of Lemma 5.2(b) and (c) are DTETFs, and since DTETFs with their parameters are unique up
to equivalence [28], their symmetry groups are known to be isomorphic to Sp(B) [28]. Their symmetry groups are
thus the affine symplectic groups of [38] that were discussed in the proof of Theorem 5.6 above. That is, no other
symmetries of them remain to be discovered.


                                                                       22
5.3. The binders of the doubly transitive equiangular tight frames of Lemma 5.2
     By Theorem 5.6, the ETFs of Lemma 5.2(b) and (c) are DTETFs. By Theorem 3.5, the binders of these ETFs
are thus either empty or form BIBDs. For any ETF that belongs to one of these four families, we now determine
which of these two phenomena occur, and moreover the parameters of any BIBD that does arise in this fashion. As
we shall see, the binders of 𝜱 D c and 𝜳 b D are empty except in a finite number of cases. In contrast, the binders of
their respective duals 𝜱b D c and 𝜳 D always form BIBDs, and these BIBDs are related to the binder of 𝜳 —the set of
all affine Lagrangian subspaces of V—in nontrivial ways.
     Recall that in general, we say that a finite sequence (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ K of vectors in a Hilbert space is a regular simplex if
it is an ETF for a space of dimension #(K) − 1. Notably, whether this occurs is independent of what if any set N
we regard to be a superset of K. As such, if 𝜱 0 = (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N0 is an ETF that is a proper subsequence of another ETF
𝜱 = (𝝋 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N —as is the case for the ETFs (𝝋𝒗 )𝒗 ∈ D c and (𝝍 𝒗 )𝒗 ∈ D of Lemma 5.2—then a subset K of N belongs to
bin(𝜱 0 ) if and only if it both belongs to bin(𝜱) and is contained in N0 . In particular, bin(𝜱 0 ) ⊆ bin(𝜱), and if 𝜱
has an empty binder then 𝜱 0 does as well. Combining this observation with Theorem 4.8 makes short work of the
problem of calculating the binders of some of the ETFs of Lemma 5.2:
Theorem 5.7. Under the assumptions of Lemma 5.2, bin(𝜱 D c ) is empty unless 𝐽 = 2. When 𝐽 = 2, 𝜱 D c is a
6-vector regular simplex and so bin(𝜱 D c ) = {D c }, while bin(𝜱) consists of all shifts of D c , which forms a BIBD
with parameters (𝑉, 𝐾, Λ, 𝑅, 𝐵) = (16, 6, 2, 6, 16).

Proof. By Lemma 5.2, 𝐽 ≥ 2 and 𝜱 is equivalent to the symplectic ETF that arises as the 𝑃 = 2 case of Definition 4.1,
and so these two ETFs have the same binder. When 𝐽 > 2, Theorem 4.8 gives that 𝜱 has an empty binder and so
the same is true for its sub-ETF 𝜱 D c . So let 𝐽 = 2 for the remainder of this proof. In that context, Lemma 5.2(b)
gives that 𝜱 D c is a 6-vector ETF for a space of dimension 5, and so is a regular simplex. As detailed in Example 3.6,
its binder thus consists of the single set D c . Here, Theorem 4.8 gives that bin(𝜱) forms a BIBD and consists of all
shifts of every set of the form (18) where (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ) is any symplectic basis for V. Fix any such basis, let 𝑽 be its
synthesis map, and consider the quadratic form Q = Q2,− ◦ 𝑽 −1 obtained by composing the canonical quadratic form
on F42 of negative type (28) with 𝑽 −1 , and so

                       Q(𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 + 𝑥3 𝒗 3 + 𝑥4 𝒗 4 ) = 𝑥 1 𝑥2 + 𝑥3 𝑥 4 + 𝑥32 + 𝑥 42 ,   ∀ 𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑥3 , 𝑥4 ∈ F2 .

The quadric Q of Q is the set given in (18). Since any symplectic basis for V is the image of (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ) under
some 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B), every set of the form (18) can be expressed as

                 { 𝑨0, 𝑨𝒗 1 , 𝑨𝒗 2 , 𝑨𝒗 1 + 𝑨𝒗 2 + 𝑨𝒗 3 , 𝑨𝒗 1 + 𝑨𝒗 2 + 𝑨𝒗 4 , 𝑨𝒗 1 + 𝑨𝒗 2 + 𝑨𝒗 3 + 𝑨𝒗 4 } = 𝑨Q.

Since Lemma 5.5 moreover gives 𝑨Q = Q + 𝒗 𝑨 , every such set is a shift of Q. Thus, bin(𝜱) consists of all shifts of
sets which are themselves shifts of Q, and is thus {𝒗 + Q : 𝒗 ∈ V}. As noted in Lemma 5.3, these shifts are distinct,
and so bin(𝜳 ) forms a BIBD with 𝑉 = 16, 𝐾 = 6 and 𝐵 = 16, which in turn implies via (9) that 𝑅 = 6 and Λ = 2.
Since 𝜱 D c is a regular simplex, D c is one of these shifts of Q, and so bin(𝜱) in fact consists of all shifts of D c . □
      To calculate the binders of 𝜱
                                  b D c and 𝜳 D we need some more facts from finite symplectic geometry. A subset S of
V is said to be totally singular with respect to some quadratic form Q that yields B when Q(𝒗) = 0 for all 𝒗 ∈ S, that
is, when S is a subset of the corresponding quadric Q. Any totally singular subspace S is totally orthogonal: for any
𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S, combining (20) with the fact that 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ∈ S gives B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = Q(𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) + Q(𝒗 1 ) + Q(𝒗 2 ) = 0 + 0 + 0 = 0.
We caution however that, in contrast with Lemma 4.4(a), a totally singular set might contain a symplectic pair, and when
it does, its span is not totally singular. Some partial converses to this fact—some from [38] and others folklore—are
given in the following result, which we reprove in the appendix.

Lemma 5.8 ([38]). Let B be a symplectic form on a vector space V over F2 of dimension 2𝐽, and let Q be the
quadric (23) of a quadratic form Q that yields B.
  (a) If S is a totally orthogonal subspace of V then S ∩ Q is a totally singular subspace. Here if S ⊈ Q then S ∩ Q
      is a subgroup of S of index 2, and S\Q is its nonidentity coset.


                                                                       23
  (b) If sign(Q) < 0 then no Lagrangian subspace of V is totally singular, and any totally singular subspace of V of
      dimension 𝐽 − 1 is contained in exactly three Lagrangian subspaces of V.
  (c) If sign(Q) > 0 then every Lagrangian subspace of V has a unique coset that is totally singular, and any totally
      singular subspace of V of dimension 𝐽 − 1 is contained in exactly three Lagrangian subspaces of V. Two of
      these three subspaces are totally singular while the third is not.
    As we now explain, these facts imply that in the context of Lemma 5.2, the BIBD formed by the binder of the
dual 𝜳 of the symplectic-equivalent ETF 𝜱, namely the set of all affine Lagrangian subspaces of V, decomposes into
three BIBDs, including one whose blocks form the binder of its sub-ETF 𝜳 D , as well as another—with twice-repeated
blocks—whose distinct blocks form the binder of the dual 𝜱  b D c of the sub-ETF 𝜱 D c of 𝜱. Though these BIBDs are
not new [38], the relationships identified here between them and these ETFs seem to be novel.

Proof. By Lemma 5.2(a), 𝜳 is equivalent to the dual of the symplectic ETF that arises as the 𝑃 = 2 case of
Definition 4.1, and so these two ETFs have the same binder. By Theorem 4.5, this binder is the set of all affine
Lagrangian subspaces of V, which forms a BIBD whose parameters are given by (33). The binder of its sub-ETF 𝜳 D
thus indeed consists of the members of bin(𝜱) that lie (completely) in D. Since D is an affine quadric of cardinality
2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1), Lemma 5.3 gives that it is of the form 𝒗 D + Q+ where Q+ is the quadric of some quadratic form Q+ that
yields B with sign(Q+ ) > 0. (In fact, such a Q+ and Q+ exist for any of the 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1) choices of 𝒗 D in D.) As such,
for any Lagrangian subspace L of V, the cosets of it that lie in D are in one-to-one correspondence with the cosets of
it that lie in Q+ . Since Lemma 5.8(c) gives that exactly one coset of the latter type exists, exactly one of the former type
exists as well. In particular, the number 𝐵 D of members of bin(𝜳 D ) is the number of Lagrangian subspaces of V.
This is the number of affine Lagrangian subspaces of V that contain 0, namely the “𝑅” parameter of (33). Since 𝜳 D
is a DTETF by Theorem 5.6, the fact that its binder is nonempty implies by Theorem 3.5 that it forms a BIBD. Here
the vertex set is the index set D of the ETF 𝜳 D which has size 𝑉D = 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1). Since any member of bin(𝜳 D ) is
also a member of bin(𝜳 ), the “𝐾” parameters of these two BIBDs are identical, and so K D = 2 𝐽 . The “𝑅” and “Λ”
parameters of this BIBD then follow from (9). Indeed,

implying S = L𝑖 ∩ Q − and so

𝒗 2 + L2 and D yields L1 , L2 and Q+ := 𝒗 D + D. Under this shift, (𝒗 1 + L1 ) ∩ D = (𝒗 2 + L2 ) ∩ D becomes
L1 ∩ Q+ = L2 ∩ Q+ , while the fact that 𝒗 1 + L1 , 𝒗 2 + L2 ⊈ D implies that L1 and L2 are not totally singular with
respect to Q+ . Here, Lemma 5.8(a) gives that S := L1 ∩ Q+ = L2 ∩ Q+ is a totally singular subspace of dimension
𝐽 − 1, at which point Lemma 5.8(c) gives that S is contained in exactly one Lagrangian subspace of V that is not
totally singular with respect to Q+ . Thus, L1 = L2 . Adding 𝒗 D to this equation gives 𝒗 1 + L1 = 𝒗 2 + L2 as claimed.
As such, for any distinct 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ D, the number of members of (37) that contain both 𝒗 1 and 𝒗 2 is independent of
one’s choice of such 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , being the number Λ − Λ D of members of bin(𝜳 ) that contain 𝒗 1 and 𝒗 2 less the number
of members of bin(𝜳 D ) that do so. The set (37) of 𝐾0 := 2 𝐽 −1 -element subsets of D thus forms a BIBD over the
V0 := 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1)-element vertex set D whose “Λ”, “𝑅” and “𝐵” parameters are obtained by subtracting those
of (34) from those of (33), as summarized in (38).

Example 5.10. We now consider instances of Lemma 5.2 and Theorems 5.7 and 5.9 where 𝐽 = 2. Up to isomorphism,
F42 is the only vector space over F2 of dimension 2𝐽 = 4,

                              B((𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑥3 , 𝑥4 ), (𝑦 1 , 𝑦 2 , 𝑦 3 , 𝑦 4 )) = (𝑥 1 𝑦 2 + 𝑥2 𝑦 1 ) + (𝑥3 𝑦 4 + 𝑥4 𝑦 3 )

is the symplectic form on it, and

                      Q+ (𝑥 1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑥3 , 𝑥4 ) := 𝑥1 𝑥2 + 𝑥 3 𝑥4 ,     Q− (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑥3 , 𝑥4 ) := 𝑥1 𝑥 2 + 𝑥3 𝑥4 + 𝑥32 + 𝑥42 ,     (44)

are the quadratic forms that yield this symplectic form via (20) of positive and negative sign (27), respectively. The
quadrics (23) of these quadratic forms are

                           Q+ := {0000, 0001, 0010, 0100, 0101, 0110, 1000, 1001, 1010, 1111},
                           Q − := {0000, 0100, 1000, 1101, 1110, 1111},                                                            (45)

respectively. By Lemma 5.3, the 16 shifts of Q+ and Q − are the 32 affine quadrics of V. Each is either the quadric of
some quadratic form that yields B (that equates to one of the two forms above via a change of basis) or the complement
of such a quadric. In particular, Q −c is a shift of Q+ , and so is one of the 16 choices of D that yield six ETFs via
Lemma 5.2. Letting D = Q −c in particular yields the four ETFs of Figure 1, and two others arise as the dual of
two of these. Specifically, the dual ETFs 𝜱 and 𝜳 of Lemma 5.2(a) arise by extracting the ten D-indexed and six
D c -indexed rows, respectively, of the character table 𝜞 of F42 with respect to B, namely the 16 × 16 matrix (14) with
its rows and columns depicted in Figure 1 in “D c then D” order. These ETFs are equivalent to—and so have the same
binders as—the symplectic ETF and its dual, respectively, (Definition 4.1) that arise from this B. In particular, by
                                                                                      c

                                   4
affine Lagrangian subspaces of F2 , as detailed in Example 4.6, which forms a BIBD with (𝑉, 𝐾, Λ, 𝑅, 𝐵) parameters
(16, 4, 3, 15, 60). Phasing the incidence matrix of this BIBD à la (10) (bottom right matrix of Figure 1) yields an ETF
that is equivalent to its dual 𝜱. Meanwhile 𝜱 (lower left matrix of Figure 1) is a 16-vector ETF for R D  R10 . Though
a symplectic-equivalent ETF such as this usually has an empty binder (Theorem 4.8) this 𝑃 = 2, 𝐽 = 2 case is unique:
by Theorem 5.7, its binder consists of all F42 shifts of D c , and it forms a BIBD with parameters (16, 6, 2, 6, 16). Its
phased incidence matrix (upper right matrix of Figure 1) is equivalent to 𝜳 . By Theorem 3.5, any block of either of
these two BIBDs is an oval of the other, that is, the support of any row of one incidence matrix has either 0 or 2 indices
in common with the support of any row of the other.
     By Lemma 5.2(b), the D c -indexed columns of 𝜱 (the six leftmost columns of the lower left matrix of Figure 1)
form a 6-vector ETF 𝜱 D c for its 5-dimensional span. By Theorem 5.7, this is an exceptional case of an ETF that
typically has an empty binder: here since 𝜱 D c is a simplex, its binder consists of the single set D c (the first row of
the upper right matrix of Figure 1). By Lemma 5.2(c), the D-index columns of 𝜳 (the ten rightmost columns of the
upper left matrix of Figure 1) form a 10-vector ETF 𝜳 D for its 5-dimensional span. By Theorem 5.9, each of the 15
Lagrangian subspaces of F42 has a unique coset that lies in D, and are the members of the binder of 𝜳 D , which forms

of the canonical quadratic form (44) on F42 of negative sign. The 10 rightmost columns of 𝜳 and 6 leftmost columns of 𝜱 form the ETFs 𝜳 D and
𝜱 D c , respectively. These ETFs are DTETFs (Theorem 5.6) and have nontrivial binders (Theorems 5.7 and 5.9) which necessarily form BIBDs
(Theorem 3.5). Phasing these BIBDs à la (10) yields ETFs equivalent to their duals. By Theorem 5.9, bin(𝜳 ) (bottom right matrix) decomposes
into three copies of bin(𝜱
                         b D c ) (upper left block), bin(𝜳 D ) (lower right block) and a third BIBD (upper right block).

a BIBD with parameters (10, 4, 2, 6, 15). Theorem 5.9 moreover gives that each of the remaining 60 − 15 = 45 affine
Lagrangian subspaces of F42 are split perfectly in half by D, and that taking the D c -components of these subspaces
yields exactly three copies of the binder of the dual 𝜱 b D c of 𝜱 D c , which in this case forms a BIBD with parameters
(6, 2, 1, 5, 15). In particular, the binders of both 𝜳 D and 𝜱b D c appear in that of 𝜳 (as the upper left block and lower
right block, respectively, of the lower right matrix of Figure 1).
    Having calculated the binder of any ETF that lies in five of the six families of Lemma 5.2, we now do the same for
the sixth. Remarkably, these binders are empty except in three cases. Our argument relies on the following technical
lemma concerning quadratic forms over F2 . Since such forms are classical, this lemma is unlikely to be original, and
so we relegate its proof to the appendix.
Lemma 5.11. Let Q be the quadric of a quadratic form Q that yields a symplectic form B on a vector space V over
F2 of dimension 2𝐽. If 𝐽 ≥ 1, the number of hyperbolic pairs (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) in V is

                                                 22𝐽 −2 [2 𝐽 −1 + sign(Q)] [2 𝐽 − sign(Q)].                                                    (46)

Moreover, for any such (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ), a vector in Q is nonorthogonal to both 𝒗 1 and 𝒗 2 if and only if it is of the form
𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 where 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 \Q0 , where Q0 is the quadric of the restriction of Q to V0 := {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }⊥ .
     If 𝐽 ≥ 2, the number of ordered pairwise nonorthogonal 4-tuples (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ) of members of Q is

                                          24𝐽 −6 [22𝐽 −2 − 1] [2 𝐽 −2 − sign(Q)] [2 𝐽 − sign(Q)].                                              (47)

Moreover, for any such (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ), a vector in Q is nonorthogonal to 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 and 𝒗 4 if and only if it is of the form
𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 3 + 𝒗 4 + 𝒗 00 where 𝒗 00 lies in the quadric Q00 of the restriction Q00 of Q to the space V00 := {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 }⊥
of dimension 2𝐽 − 4, which has sign(Q00 ) = − sign(Q).
Theorem 5.12. Under the assumptions of Lemma 5.2, bin(𝜳   b D ) is empty when 𝐽 > 4. When instead 𝐽 ∈ {2, 3, 4}, it
forms a BIBD with the following (𝑉, 𝐾, Λ, 𝑅, 𝐵) parameters:

             𝐽 = 2 : (10, 4, 2, 6, 15),            𝐽 = 3 : (36, 6, 8, 56, 336),              𝐽 = 4 : (136, 10, 64, 960, 13056).

In these three cases, every member of either bin(𝜳 D ) or bin(𝜳
                                                              b D ) is an oval of the other.

Proof. By Theorem 5.6, the dual ETFs 𝜳 D and 𝜳    b D of Lemma 5.2(c) are DTETFs. By Theorem 5.9, bin(𝜳 D ) forms
a BIBD. By Theorem 3.5, bin(𝜳  b D ) is either empty or forms a BIBD and, in the latter case, every member of either
bin(𝜳 D ) or bin(𝜳
                 b D ) is an oval of the other. Here, Lemma 5.2(c) gives 𝑆 = ∥ 𝝍 b𝒗 ∥ 2 = 2 𝐽 −1 + 1 for all 𝒗 ∈ D and

to determine when bin(𝜳 D ) is nonempty—and if so, the parameters of the resulting BIBD—we can take 𝒗 0 ∈ D to
                             b
assume without loss of generality that 0 ∈ D. Since D is an affine quadric of size 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1) that contains 0, it is
the quadric Q of some positive quadratic form that yields B. Moreover, since bin(𝜳     b D ) is a BIBD if it is nonempty,
it suffices to determine the number 𝑅 of subsets K of D = Q that both satisfy (48) and contain 0: if 𝑅 = 0, then
bin(𝜳b D ) is empty, while if 𝑅 > 0, then (9) gives Λ = 𝑅 (𝐾 −1) and 𝐵 = 𝑉 𝑅 where 𝑉 = #(D) = 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1) and

𝐾 = #(K) = 2 𝐽 −1 + 2. Note that if K ⊆ D satisfies (48), then taking 𝒗 3 = 0 in (48) implies that S = K\{0} satisfies

                              S ⊆ Q,        #(S) = 2 𝐽 −1 + 1,         B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1, ∀ 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S, 𝒗 1 ≠ 𝒗 2 .                         (49)

Conversely, if S is any set that satisfies (49), then K = S ⊔ {0} satisfies (48): clearly 0 ∉ S, and moreover
B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) + B(𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 ) + B(𝒗 3 , 𝒗 1 ) = 1 for any pairwise distinct 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 ∈ K, being either 1 + 1 + 1 when all three of
                                                                         29
these vectors are nonzero, and otherwise being a sum of one 1 and two values of 0. As such, 𝑅 is moreover the number
of sets S that satisfy (49). If 𝑅 > 0, then such a set S exists, and Theorem 4.8 implies that 2 𝐽 −1 + 1 = #(S) ≤ 2𝐽 + 1,
that is, 2 𝐽 −2 ≤ 𝐽, namely 𝐽 ≤ 4. By the contrapositive statement, bin(𝜳        b D ) is thus empty when 𝐽 > 4.
     In summary, it thus now suffices to assume that 𝐽 is 2, 3 or 4, and show that the number 𝑅 of sets S that satisfy (49)
is 6, 56 or 960, respectively. Theorem 4.16 of [38] gives the parameters of these ovoid designs without proof. For the
sake of completeness, we count these S explicitly. To do so, note that there are exactly (2 𝐽 −1 + 1)! ways to enumerate
the members of any such set S to obtain an ordered finite sequence (𝒗 𝑖 )𝑖=1        2 𝐽 −1 +1 in Q that satisfies B(𝒗 , 𝒗 ) = 1 for all
                                                                                                                       𝑖1 𝑖2
distinct 𝑖1 , 𝑖2 . As such, we obtain 𝑅 by dividing the number of such sequences by (2 𝐽 −1 + 1)!.
     When 𝐽 = 2, taking 𝐽 = 2 and sign(Q) > 0 in (46) of Lemma 5.11 gives 36 hyperbolic pairs (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) in V, and
moreover that for any such (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ), a vector 𝒗 3 ∈ Q is nonorthogonal to both 𝒗 1 and 𝒗 2 if and only if 𝒗 3 = 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0
where 𝒗 0 belongs to the singleton set V0 \Q0 of vectors that are nonsingular with respect to a positive quadratic form
on a symplectic space over F2 of dimension 2. There are thus 36 ways to choose three ordered pairwise nonorthogonal
members (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 ) of Q. Dividing this number by 3! = 6 gives that there are exactly 𝑅 = 6 sets S that satisfy (49)
when 𝐽 = 2, as claimed.
     Similarly, when 𝐽 = 3, taking 𝐽 = 3 and sign(Q) > 0 in (47) of Lemma 5.11 gives 6720 ways to choose four
ordered pairwise nonorthogonal members (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ) of Q, and moreover that the number of ways to append a fifth
such vector 𝒗 5 to this tuple is the number of vectors that are singular with respect to a negative quadratic form over a
symplectic space over F2 of dimension 2, namely 1. As such, dividing (6720) (1) = 6720 by 5! = 120 gives that there
are exactly 𝑅 = 56 sets S that satisfy (49) when 𝐽 = 3, as claimed.
     When 𝐽 = 4, taking 𝐽 = 4 and sign(Q) > 0 in (47) of Lemma 5.11 gives 2903040 ways to choose four ordered
pairwise nonorthogonal members (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ) of Q. Moreover, for any (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ), taking 𝐽 = 2 and sign(Q) < 0
in (47) of Lemma 5.11 gives 120 ways to choose (𝒗 5 , 𝒗 6 , 𝒗 7 , 𝒗 8 ) so that the members of (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 , 𝒗 5 , 𝒗 6 , 𝒗 7 , 𝒗 8 )
lie in Q and are pairwise nonorthogonal. For any such (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 , 𝒗 5 , 𝒗 6 , 𝒗 7 , 𝒗 8 ), Lemma 5.11 further gives that the
number of ways to append a ninth such vector 𝒗 9 to this tuple is the number of vectors that are singular with respect
to a positive quadratic form over a trivial space, namely 1. As such, dividing (2903040) (120) (1) = 348364800 by
9! = 362880 gives that there are exactly 𝑅 = 960 sets S that satisfy (49) when 𝐽 = 4, as claimed.                                           □
    We now summarize the parameters of the BIBDs formed by the binders of the six DTETFs of Lemma 5.2 when
𝐽 ∈ {2, 3, 4}. Here “𝐷” is the dimension of the ETF’s span, “𝑉” is the number of vectors in the ETF, “𝐾” is the
necessary number of vectors in any regular simplex that it contains, “Λ” is the number of such regular simplices that
contain any given pair of distinct ETF vectors, “𝑅” is the number of such regular simplices that contain any given ETF
vector, and “𝐵” is the total number of such regular simplices. In particular, we denote that a binder is empty here by
giving Λ, 𝑅 and 𝐵 to be 0. Letting 𝐽 be 2, 3 and 4 in Lemma 5.2 and Theorems 5.7, 5.9 and 5.12 yields the following
three tables, respectively:

For all larger values of 𝐽, the binders of 𝜱, 𝜱 D c and 𝜳
                                                        b D are all empty, while the binders of 𝜳 , 𝜱b D c and 𝜳 D form
BIBDs whose parameters are given in Theorem 5.9. A number of these entries, such as those for 𝜳 and 𝜳          b D when
𝐽 = 2, and those for 𝜱  b D c and 𝜳
                                  b D when 𝐽 = 3, are consistent with those previously published in the literature [16]
based upon numerical evidence. That said, the BinderFinder algorithm of [16] is prohibitively expensive for the
instances of these ETFs where 𝐽 ≥ 4, and so for example, it is a novel observation that this 120-vector DTETF for R35
has the property that any two of its vectors are contained in exactly 45 regular simplices.
     For 𝐽 ∈ {2, 3, 4}, any member of the binder of either 𝜳 D or 𝜳
                                                                  b D is an oval of the BIBD formed by the other. This
is true even when 𝐽 = 2 and the two resulting BIBDs have the same parameters (10, 4, 2, 6, 15). This can be explicitly
verified, for example, by noting that the support of any row of the right block of the upper right matrix of Figure 1 has

                                                                     30
either exactly two or zero vertices in common with those of the lower right block of the lower right matrix there. (We
caution here that 𝜱 D := (𝝋𝒗 )𝒗 ∈ D , the 𝐽 = 2 instance of which appears in Figure 1 as the 10 rightmost columns of 𝜱, is
not an ETF. In general, the dual of a sub-ETF does not arise as a submatrix of a dual ETF. That said, the Gram matrices
of two such finite sequences of vectors agree in their off-diagonal entries, and so every member of bin(𝜱) ∩ D of the
appropriate cardinality is also a member of bin(𝜳    b D ).) When 𝐽 = 2, bin(𝜱) and bin(𝜳 ) also consist of ovals of the
other, as do bin(𝜱 D c ) and bin(𝜱 D c ).
                                  b
     To date, we have not discovered an infinite family of DTETFs that have the property that the binders of both them
and their duals are nonempty. By Theorem 3.5, any member of either BIBD formed by these binders is necessarily
an oval of the other. In light of the 𝐽 = 2 instance of 𝜳 D or 𝜳 b D , one possible candidate for such a family consists of
    2                                                       1  2
(𝑄 + 1)-vector DTETFs for real spaces of dimension 2 (𝑄 + 1), where 𝑄 is any odd prime power [28]. We leave a
deeper investigation of the binders of such DTETFs, as well as those of others [28, 12], for future work.
     From the point of view of deterministic compressed sensing, perhaps the most exciting contribution of this work
is the revelation of Theorem 5.12 that the binder of the DTETF Ψ    b D of Lemma 5.2(c) is empty whenever 𝐽 > 4. Here
recall that the spark of an ETF outperforms Donoho and Elad’s spark bound (1) precisely when its binder is empty.
Though, as noted above, this applies to 𝜱, 𝜱 D c and 𝜳  b D whenever 𝐽 is not small, the ETFs 𝜱 and 𝜱 D c are of limited
applicability to compressed sensing since the dimension of their spans is more than half of the number of their vectors:
why bother with the complexities of compressed sensing when one can simply perform traditional sensing at no more
than twice the cost? In contrast, the parameters of 𝜳    b D match those of certain Tremain ETFs [17], and as 𝐽 grows
                                    1 𝐽 −1
large, the dimension of its span 3 (2        + 1) (2 + 1) is about one-third of the number 2 𝐽 −1 (2 𝐽 + 1) of its vectors.
                                                    𝐽

This begs the question, What is the spark of 𝜳    b D ? More importantly, to what degree does 𝜳   b D satisfy the restricted
isometry property? Though such problems seem to be computationally intractable in general [1, 3], we have already
seen, in Theorem 5.9 for example, that it is possible to exactly compute the spark of any ETF that belongs to certain
infinite families of them by showing that their binders are nonempty. Here though 𝜳   b D has an empty binder, it is highly
symmetric—being a DTETF—and its Gram matrix (Lemma 5.2(c)) is elegantly expressed in terms of a quadratic form
over F2 . By using sufficiently sophisticated algebra and finite geometry, can one for example also exactly compute the
spark of 𝜳 b D for all 𝐽 > 4?

Appendix A. Proof of Lemma 3.2
    (a) For any 𝑣 1 , 𝑣 2 , 𝑣 3 , 𝑣 4 ∈ V such that 𝑣 1 ≠ 𝑣 2 and 𝑣 3 ≠ 𝑣 4 , taking 𝜎 ∈ Aut(V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) such that 𝜎(𝑣 1 ) = 𝑣 3
and 𝜎(𝑣 2 ) = 𝑣 4 , the corresponding permutation 𝜏 of B maps {𝑏 ∈ B : 𝑣 1 , 𝑣 2 ∈ K𝑏 } onto {𝑏 ′ ∈ B : 𝑣 3 , 𝑣 4 ∈ K𝑏′ },
implying that the number of blocks which contain 𝑣 1 and 𝑣 2 is the same as that which contain 𝑣 3 and 𝑣 4 .
    (b) Let L be an 𝐿-element arc of a BIBD (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ). Recall that the incidence matrix 𝑿 ∈ R B×V of this
BIBD satisfies (8). Letting 𝝌 L be the characteristic function of L, we also have ( 𝑿 𝜒 L ) (𝑏) = #(K𝑏 ∩ L) ∈ {0, 1, 2}
for all 𝑏 ∈ B. Moreover, letting 𝑁1 and 𝑁2 be the number of blocks K𝑏 that intersect L in exactly one or two vertices,
respectively, (8) implies
                            ∑︁
           𝑁1 + 2𝑁2 =             #(K𝑏 ∩ L) = ⟨1 B , 𝑿 𝝌 L ⟩ = ⟨𝑿 T 1 B , 𝝌 L ⟩ = ⟨𝑅1 V , 𝝌 L ⟩ = 𝑅𝐿,
                           𝑏∈ B
                           ∑︁
           𝑁1 + 4𝑁2 =             [#(K𝑏 ∩ L)] 2 = ∥ 𝑿 𝝌 L ∥ 2 = ⟨ 𝝌 L , [(𝑅 − Λ)I + Λ1 V ×V ] 𝝌 L ⟩ = (𝑅 − Λ)𝐿 + Λ𝐿 2 .
                           𝑏∈ B

Subtracting the second equation here from twice the first and then recalling (9) gives

                                                                𝑅                   𝑉 −1
                                                                  + 1) − 𝐿] = Λ𝐿 [( 𝐾 −1 + 1) − 𝐿].                                        (A.1)
                   𝑉 −1                        𝑉 −1
In particular, 𝐿 ≤ 𝐾 −1 + 1. Moreover, if 𝐿 = 𝐾 −1 + 1 then 𝐿 > 1 and (A.1) gives 𝑁1 = 0, that is, #(K𝑏 ∩ L) ∈ {0, 2}
for all 𝑏 ∈ B. Conversely, if L is any nonempty subset of V such that #(K𝑏 ∩ L) ∈ {0, 2} for all 𝑏 ∈ B then L is an
                                                                        𝑉 −1
arc of this BIBD with 𝐿 > 0 and 𝑁1 = 0, implying by (A.1) that 𝐿 = 𝐾      −1 + 1. When this occurs, 𝐾 − 1 necessarily
divides 𝑉 − 1. See [2] for an alternative upper bound on the size of an arc that holds when 𝐾 − 1 ∤ 𝑉 − 1.
    (c) Assume that (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) is doubly transitive—implying by (a) that it is a BIBD—and moreover that this
BIBD has an oval, namely that there exists a nonempty subset L of V such that #(K𝑏 ∩ L) ∈ {0, 2} for all 𝑏 ∈ B.
Any automorphism 𝜎 of (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) maps any such oval L to another: if 𝜏 is the associated permutation of B,
                       #(K𝑏 ∩ 𝜎(L)) = #(𝜎(𝜎 −1 (K𝑏 ) ∩ L)) = #(K 𝜏 −1 (𝑏) ∩ L) ∈ {0, 2},                       ∀ 𝑏 ∈ B.
                                                                       32
Letting (L 𝑎 ) 𝑎∈ A be any enumeration of the set of all ovals of (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ), this implies that Aut(V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) is a
subgroup of Aut(V, (L 𝑎 ) 𝑎∈ A ). Since (V, (K𝑏 ) 𝑏∈ B ) is doubly transitive this implies that (V, (L 𝑎 ) 𝑎∈ A ) is as well,
and so by (a) is itself a BIBD. Since #(K𝑏 ∩ L 𝑎 ) ∈ {0, 2} for all 𝑎 ∈ A and 𝑏 ∈ B, any member of either BIBD is an
oval of the other.

Appendix B. Proof of Lemma 3.4
    Consider the matrix 𝑨 ∈ F N× N defined by
                                ∑︁
                 𝑨(𝑛1 , 𝑛3 ) :=    ⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛2 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛2 , 𝝋 𝑛3 ⟩⟨𝝋 𝑛3 , 𝝋 𝑛1 ⟩ = (𝜱 ∗𝜱) 2 (𝑛1 , 𝑛3 )(𝜱 ∗𝜱) (𝑛1 , 𝑛3 ).                        (B.1)
                                      𝑛2 ∈ N

By assumption, for any 𝑛1 , 𝑛3 , 𝑛4 , 𝑛6 ∈ N with 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛3 and 𝑛4 ≠ 𝑛6 , there exists 𝜎 ∈ Sym(𝜱) such that 𝜎(𝑛1 ) = 𝑛4
and 𝜎(𝑛3 ) = 𝑛6 . Since 𝜎 ∈ Sym(𝜱), there exists a finite sequence of unimodular scalars (𝑧 𝑛 ) 𝑛∈ N such that
⟨𝝋 𝜎 (𝑛7 ) , 𝝋 𝜎 (𝑛8 ) ⟩ = 𝑧 𝑛7 𝑧 𝑛8 ⟨𝝋 𝑛7 , 𝝋 𝑛8 ⟩ for all 𝑛7 , 𝑛8 ∈ N . In particular, |⟨𝝋 𝑛4 , 𝝋 𝑛6 ⟩| = |⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛3 ⟩| for all such
𝑛1 , 𝑛3 , 𝑛4 , 𝑛6 , implying there exists 𝐶 ≥ 0 such that |⟨𝝋 𝑛1 , 𝝋 𝑛3 ⟩| = 𝐶 when 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛3 . In fact, 𝐶 > 0 since 𝑁 > 𝐷, and
we without loss of generality scale 𝜱 so that 𝐶 = 1. Similarly, (11) implies that 𝑨(𝑛4 , 𝑛6 ) = 𝑨(𝑛1 , 𝑛3 ) for all such
𝑛1 , 𝑛3 , 𝑛4 , 𝑛6 , and so there exists 𝐴 ∈ F such that 𝑨(𝑛1 , 𝑛3 ) = 𝐴 when 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛3 . Multiplying (B.1) by (𝜱 ∗𝜱) (𝑛1 , 𝑛3 ) thus
gives (𝜱 ∗𝜱) 2 (𝑛1 , 𝑛3 ) = 𝐴(𝜱 ∗𝜱) (𝑛1 , 𝑛3 ) when 𝑛1 ≠ 𝑛3 . When instead 𝑛1 = 𝑛3 , (𝜱 ∗𝜱) 2 (𝑛1 , 𝑛3 ) = 𝑆 2 + (𝑁 − 1) while
(𝜱 ∗𝜱) (𝑛1 , 𝑛3 ) = 𝑆. Altogether, (𝜱 ∗𝜱) 2 = 𝐴(𝜱 ∗𝜱) + [𝑆(𝑆 − 𝐴) + (𝑁 − 1)]I. That said, ker(𝜱 ∗𝜱) contains a nonzero
vector since 𝑁 > 𝐷, and applying this above equation to it gives 𝑆(𝑆 − 𝐴) + (𝑁 − 1) = 0. Thus, (𝜱 ∗𝜱) 2 = 𝐴(𝜱 ∗𝜱),
implying 𝜱 is a tight frame and so also an ETF.

Appendix E. Proof of Lemma 5.4

    When 𝐽 = 1, V has exactly three nonzero vectors, any two of which form a singular pair, implying Sp(B) = GL(V).
Here, there is just one quadratic form Q that yields B of negative sign, having quadric Q = {0}, and the action of
Sp(B) on the set of all such forms is doubly transitive in a vacuous sense. There are three quadratic forms that yield B
of positive sign, each uniquely defined by its nonsingular vector, which may be any nonzero vector in V. Here, (30) is
doubly transitive since any pair of distinct nonzero vectors in V can be mapped to any other such pair via a member
       Î =−2GL(V). As such, we assume 𝐽 ≥ 2 for the remainder of the proof. When 𝐽 = 2, all products below of the
of Sp(B)
form “ 𝐽𝑗=1   ” should be regarded as 1.
    Let Q be a quadratic form that yields B with desired sign (27). By (22), the quadratic forms that yield B are
parameterized by 𝒗 𝑳 ∈ V, being of the form 𝒗 ↦→ Q𝑳 (𝒗) := Q(𝒗) + B(𝒗 𝑳 , 𝒗). By (27), sign(Q𝑳 ) = sign(Q) if and
only if the quadric Q 𝑳 of Q𝑳 has the same size as the quadric Q of Q. By (29), this occurs if and only if 𝒗 𝑳 ∈ Q. The
number of quadratic forms that yield B and have the same sign as Q is thus #(Q) = 2 𝐽 −1 [2 𝐽 + sign(Q)], and so

                          #(Q) − 1 = 2 𝐽 −1 [2 𝐽 + sign(Q)] − 1 = [2 𝐽 −1 + sign(Q)] [2 𝐽 − sign(Q)].                                 (E.1)

The number of ordered pairs (Q1 , Q2 ) of distinct quadratic forms that yield B and have the same sign as Q is thus

                          #(Q) [#(Q) − 1] = 2 𝐽 −1 [2 𝐽 + sign(Q)] [2 𝐽 −1 + sign(Q)] [2 𝐽 − sign(Q)]
                                               = 2 𝐽 −1 (22𝐽 − 1) [2 𝐽 −1 + sign(Q)].                                                 (E.2)

To show that the aforementioned action ( 𝑨, Q′ ) ↦→ 𝑨 · Q′ is doubly transitive, it thus suffices to show that the size
of the orbit of any particular such pair (Q1 , Q2 ) under the induced action 𝑨 · (Q1 , Q2 ) := ( 𝑨 · Q1 , 𝑨 · Q2 ) is given
by (E.2). Here, by redefining “Q” to be Q1 , we may assume without loss of generality that (Q1 , Q2 ) = (Q, Q𝑳 ) where
𝒗 𝑳 ∈ Q and 𝒗 𝑳 ≠ 0 since sign(Q1 ) = sign(Q2 ) and Q1 ≠ Q2 , respectively.
    We compute the size of this orbit via its stabilizer. Here, recalling that 𝑨 ∈ Sp(B) if and only if 𝑨 : V → V is
linear and maps any particular symplectic basis for V to another, the order of Sp(B) is simply the number of (ordered)
symplectic bases for V. Further recall that any such basis is constructed by iteratively choosing (ordered) symplectic
pairs from the orthogonal complement of those already chosen. When V has dimension 2𝐽, any 𝒗 1 ∈ V\{0} can
serve as the first member of such a pair, and any 𝒗 2 ∈ V\{𝒗 1 }⊥ can serve as the second, implying the number of such
pairs is (22𝐽 − 1) (22𝐽 − 22𝐽 −1 ) = 22𝐽 −1 (22𝐽 − 1). Thus, Sp(B) has order

Appendix F. Proof of Lemma 5.8

     (a) We claim that if S is a totally orthogonal subspace of V then S ∩ Q is a totally singular subspace of
V. Clearly, S ∩ Q is totally singular. It contains 0 since 0 ∈ S and 0 ∈ Q. Since the underlying field is F2 ,
this immediately implies that it is closed under scalar multiplication. It is moreover closed under addition since
Q(𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) + Q(𝒗 1 ) + Q(𝒗 2 ) = 0 + 0 + 0 = 0 for all 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ∈ S ∩ Q, yielding the claim. We next claim that
any totally orthogonal subspace S of V that is not contained in Q is split perfectly in half by it. Indeed, if there exists
𝒗 0 ∈ S\Q, then Q(𝒗 + 𝒗 0 ) = B(𝒗, 𝒗 0 ) + Q(𝒗) + Q(𝒗 0 ) = 0 + Q(𝒗) + 1 for all 𝒗 ∈ S, implying 𝒗 ↦→ 𝒗 + 𝒗 0 is an involution
from S ∩ Q onto S\Q. Since S ∩ Q is a subgroup of S with S = (S ∩ Q) ⊔ (S\Q) = (S ∩ Q) ⊔ (𝒗 0 + S ∩ Q), it has
index 2 and its nonidentity coset is S\Q, yielding the claim.
     For (b) and (c), continue to let sign(Q) be arbitrary, and let S be any totally singular subspace of V of dimension
𝐽 − 1. By (a), such subspaces exist, arising for example as any (not necessarily proper) subspace of the intersection
of Q with any Lagrangian subspace L of V. Clearly, there exists 𝒗 1 ∈ S\{0} if and only if 𝐽 ≥ 2. When
this occurs, any such 𝒗 1 belongs to Q\{0} and so there exists 𝒗 2 ∈ V such that (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) is a hyperbolic pair.
Here necessarily 𝒗 2 ∈ Q\S since Q(𝒗 2 ) = 0 and B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1. Further recall that any 𝒗 ∈ V can be uniquely
decomposed as 𝒗 = 𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥 2 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 where 𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ∈ F2 and 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 := {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }⊥ . When 𝒗 ∈ S, we necessarily
have 0 = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗) = B(𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 ) = 𝑥 2 , implying 𝒗 0 = 𝒗 + 𝑥1 𝒗 1 is a linear combination of two elements of S,
and so 𝒗 0 ∈ S0 := S ∩ V0 . Thus, S = {𝑥 1 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 0 : 𝑥 1 ∈ F, 𝒗 0 ∈ S0 }. Here since 𝒗 1 ∉ V0 and so 𝒗 1 ∉ S0 , we have
dim(S0 ) = dim(S) − 1 = 𝐽 − 2. Since S is totally singular with respect to Q, S0 is totally singular with respect to its
restriction Q0 to V0 : by (25), 0 = Q(𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 0 ) = 𝑥1 (0) + Q0 (𝒗 0 ) = Q0 (𝒗 0 ) for all 𝒗 0 ∈ S0 .

note that since dim(S ⊥ ) − dim(S) = 2, S has three nonidentity cosets in S ⊥ . In light of the observations above, these

Now note that by Lemma 4.4, every Lagrangian subspace L of V is of the form L = span{S, 𝒗} where S is a
totally orthogonal subspace of dimension 𝐽 − 1 and 𝒗 ∈ S ⊥ \S. We thus have that any totally singular subspace S of
                                                                             38
dimension 𝐽 − 1 is contained in exactly three Lagrangian subspaces, namely S ⊔ S1 , S ⊔ S2 and S ⊔ S1,2 , exactly two
of which—S ⊔ S1 and S ⊔ S2 —are totally singular when sign(Q) > 0, and none of which are totally singular when
sign(Q) < 0.
     In particular, when sign(Q) < 0, no Lagrangian subspace L of V is totally singular since if it were, then any
(𝐽 − 1)-dimensional subspace S of it would be as well, implying that L is one of the three Lagrangian subspaces that
contains S, none of which are totally singular, a contradiction. This completes the proof of (b).
     For (c), now assume that sign(Q) > 0 and that L is a Lagrangian subspace of V. If L is totally singular, then
L = 0 + L is the only totally singular coset of L: if L ⊥ = L ⊆ Q and 𝒗 1 + L ⊆ Q for some 𝒗 1 ∈ V then 𝒗 1 = 𝒗 1 + 0 ∈
𝒗 1 + L ⊆ Q, and so applying (20) with any 𝒗 2 ∈ L ⊆ Q gives 0 = Q(𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 ) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) + Q(𝒗 1 ) + Q(𝒗 2 ) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ),
implying 𝒗 1 ∈ L ⊥ = L and so 𝒗 + L = L. When L is not totally singular, (a) gives that S := L ∩ Q is a totally
singular subspace of V of dimension dim(L) − 1 = 𝐽 − 1. Constructing a hyperbolic basis (𝒗 𝑘 ) 2𝐽          𝑘=1 for V such that
              −1
(𝒗 2 𝑗 −1 ) 𝐽𝑗=1 is a basis for S = L ∩ Q and defining S1 , S2 and S1,2 from S as above, the fact that L is not totally singular
gives L = S ⊔ S1,2 . There is thus indeed a coset of L that is totally singular:

                    𝒗 2𝐽 −1 + L = 𝒗 2𝐽 −1 + (S ⊔ S1,2 ) = (𝒗 2𝐽 −1 + S) ⊔ (𝒗 2𝐽 −1 + S1,2 ) = S1 ⊔ S2 ⊆ Q.

To see that this coset is unique, note that if 𝒗 1 + L ⊆ Q then 𝒗 1 ∈ Q and so 0 = Q(𝒗 1 +𝒗 2 ) = B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) +Q(𝒗 1 ) +Q(𝒗 2 ) =
B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) for any 𝒗 2 ∈ S, implying 𝒗 1 ∈ S ⊥ . At the same time, 𝒗 1 ∉ L since otherwise 𝒗 1 + L = L ⊈ Q. As such,
if 𝒗 1 + L ⊆ Q then 𝒗 1 ∈ S ⊥ \L = S1 ⊔ S2 . Now note that since {𝒗 1 ∈ V : 𝒗 1 + L ⊆ Q} is clearly closed under
shifts by members of 𝑳 it is itself a union of cosets of 𝑳. Since this set is nonempty, containing 𝒗 2𝐽 −1 , and is
contained in S1 ⊔ S2 , we thus have {𝒗 1 ∈ V : 𝒗 1 + L ⊆ Q} = S1 ⊔ S2 = 𝒗 2𝐽 −1 + L. Thus, 𝒗 1 + L ⊆ Q if and only if
𝒗 1 + L = 𝒗 2𝐽 −1 + L.

Appendix G. Proof of Lemma 5.11
    Recall that if (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) is a symplectic pair in V then any 𝒗 ∈ V uniquely decomposes as 𝒗 = 𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥 2 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0
for some 𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ∈ F2 and 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 := {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }⊥ . Further recall that when this occurs, if Q is a quadratic form on V
that yields B, then its restriction Q0 to V0 is a quadratic form that yields the restriction B0 of B to V0 and moreover
satisfies (24) for any 𝑥1 , 𝑥2 ∈ F2 and 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 .
    If 𝐽 = 1 and sign(Q) < 0, the quantity in (46) is 0, which is indeed the number of symplectic pairs (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) of
members of Q = {0}. So now assume that either 𝐽 ≥ 2 or 𝐽 = 1 and sign(Q) > 0. Here, recall that there exists
𝒗 1 ∈ Q\{0}, and moreover that for any such 𝒗 1 , there exists 𝒗 2 ∈ Q such that B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1. For any such hyperbolic
pair (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ), (24) reduces to (25), implying

                          B(𝒗 1 , 𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 ) = 𝑥2 ,   Q(𝑥1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 ) = 𝑥1 𝑥2 + Q0 (𝒗 0 ),

for all 𝑥 1 , 𝑥2 ∈ F2 , 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 . In particular, for any 𝒗 1 ∈ Q\{0}, the pair (𝒗 1 , 𝒗) is hyperbolic if and only if
𝒗 = 𝑥 1 𝒗 1 + 𝑥2 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 where 𝑥2 = 1 and 𝑥1 𝑥 2 + Q0 (𝒗 0 ) = 0. For any 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 , this holds if and only if 𝑥 1 = Q0 (𝒗 0 ) and
𝑥2 = 1. Thus, the number of hyperbolic pairs in V is the product of the number #(Q\{0}) of choices of 𝒗 1 with the
number #(V0 ) of choices for such 𝒗, namely the value given in (46):

              #(Q\{0})#(V0 ) = 2 𝐽 −1 [2 𝐽 + sign(Q)] − 1 22( 𝐽 −1) = 22𝐽 −2 [2 𝐽 −1 + sign(Q)] [2 𝐽 − sign(Q)].

For any hyperbolic pair (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) in V, now recall that sign(Q0 ) = sign(Q) and that (26) holds, namely that Q partitions
into Q0 , 𝒗 1 + Q0 , 𝒗 2 + Q0 and 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + V0 \Q0 . Here since B(𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ) = 1 and Q0 ⊆ V0 = {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }⊥ , the set
𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + V0 \Q0 is the only one of these four components whose members are nonorthogonal to both 𝒗 1 and 𝒗 2 .
Moreover, B(𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 01 , 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 02 ) = B0 (𝒗 01 , 𝒗 02 ) for any 𝒗 01 , 𝒗 02 ∈ V0 \Q0 . As such, if additional vectors in
Q are appended to (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 ), the members of the resulting tuple are pairwise nonorthogonal if and only if the appended
vectors are obtained by adding 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 to pairwise nonorthogonal vectors in V0 \Q0 .
     For the remaining claims, now let Q0 be the quadric of an arbitrary quadratic form Q0 that yields a symplectic

For any such (𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 ), a vector 𝒗 ∈ Q is nonorthogonal to both 𝒗 1 and 𝒗 2 if and only if 𝒗 = 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 0 for
some 𝒗 0 ∈ V0 \Q0 . Moreover, any such 𝒗 is nonorthogonal to both 𝒗 3 = 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 01 and 𝒗 4 = 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 02 if and
only if 𝒗 0 is nonorthogonal to both 𝒗 01 and 𝒗 02 , namely if and only if 𝒗 0 = 𝒗 01 + 𝒗 02 + 𝒗 00 for some 𝒗 00 ∈ Q00 . Thus,
𝒗 ∈ Q is nonorthogonal to 𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 and 𝒗 4 if and only if 𝒗 = 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 01 + 𝒗 02 + 𝒗 00 = 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 3 + 𝒗 4 + 𝒗 00 for
some 𝒗 00 ∈ Q00 . Here, Q0 is the restriction of Q to V0 = {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }⊥ , and Q00 is the restriction of Q0 to the orthogonal
complement of {𝒗 01 , 𝒗 02 } = {𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 1 + 𝒗 2 + 𝒗 4 } in V0 , denoted V00 . We claim that V00 = {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 }⊥ .
Indeed, if 𝒗 ∈ {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 }⊥ then 𝒗 ∈ {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }⊥ = V0 and 𝒗 ∈ {𝒗 01 , 𝒗 02 }⊥ , implying 𝒗 ∈ V00 . Conversely, if
𝒗 ∈ V00 then 𝒗 ∈ V = {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 }⊥ and 𝒗 ∈ {𝒗 01 , 𝒗 02 }⊥ , implying 𝒗 ∈ {𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 }⊥ . Thus, Q00 is the restriction of Q to
V00 = {𝒗 1 , 𝒗 2 , 𝒗 3 , 𝒗 4 }⊥ . Since V0 has codimension 2 in V and V00 has codimension 2 in V0 , this space V00 has
codimension 4 in the 2𝐽-dimensional space V. Moreover, sign(Q00 ) = − sign(Q0 ) = − sign(Q).
